Threads and pools beside master's: the connect pool (rows 10 to 12, 15, and the prevout fetch), the receive pool of five threads (row 14), the effects thread, the connector, and the four block I/O threads (4.14). The flush helpers are pool tasks. Queues: the pool's task queue, the executor's queue and its backlog wait, the receive task set, the validation-interface queue as on master. Locks: one per shard, one per shard of the window's outputs, the script-cache lock, the remembered-marker mutex, the block-index commit guard, the executor's mutex, the block-task mutex, the depot's lock and the block cache's own; added since: the connector's mutex, the window's (`WindowSync`), the flush helpers' and the published tip's. Job 2 counted eleven mutexes and four condition variables at its tip; not recounted.

What the table shows. The store (rows 9 and 10) is 4610 of 11288 lines, with window and flush (9 to 13) 5913; the architecture and crash-safety base (1 to 4, 8) is 2601 and carries no measured gain of its own; everything after the window (14 to 20) is 903. Memory outside `-dbcache` sits in rows 6, 10, 11, 14, 16 with 19, 18, and row 3 up to 64 MiB; also the pinned blocks (an account of their own, `util::ByteAccount`). Rows 15, 16 and 18 answer one cause, memory made on one thread and freed on another. No row has a ratio from a real sync against master: real figures are the branch against its own earlier tip or a sync with no master run beside it; every ratio against master is from the harness.

### 4.2 The store: entries, confirmed changes, locks

The store knows no block before it is valid. A block's spends and creates arrive in one call with the block's height, `CoinsStore::ApplyConfirmed()`, shard by shard: a shard's lock is taken once for all of the block's changes there, and the shard's buckets and nodes are asked for ahead of the first probe (`PrefetchAll()`, every cache line of a chain's first node). No entry has a state that belongs to a block still being validated, and nothing in the store is visited a second time when a block is published. A spend that finds no coin and a create that meets a coin it may not go over are not made: they are counted and, with a `ConfirmedReport`, named, and the report keeps a coin a create went over, so that the caller can take the block's other changes back (4.5). `FetchInto()` reads a block's inputs by position with one hold of each shard, and `MayHave()` answers per shard whether any of a set of outpoints can be there (the BIP30 check, 4.6).

`CoinsStore::AccessCoin()` returns the calling thread's copy, good until the thread's next call, never a reference into a shard. `GetDirtyCount()` is the shards' dirty total; `SanityCheck()` asks the entry machine whether each entry's state is allowed and checks lists, totals and heights against the entries.

Form. An entry's state is four flags (DIRTY, FRESH, COLLECTED and the flag of a coin made over an erase the database is still owed) and whether its coin is spent. `coins_entry_machine.h` names every change the store makes to an entry as an event (`BaseCopy`, `ConfirmedNew`, `ConfirmedWrite`, `BatchOverwrite`, `ConfirmedSpend`, `KeptWritten`, `CoinOverErase`, `RunWritesOwed`, `RunCollects`, `RunLands`) and lists per event the states it applies to and the bits it clears and sets; the table from (event, state) to the next state is built during compilation, which fails if two rules of an event apply to one state or a rule leads out of the allowed states. The store writes an entry's flags in one function, `Enter()`, reached through `Transit()`; an event the table refuses stops the node in every build. The machine also answers the height an entry is filed under: the height of the change, or the lower one of an erase the entry still owes the database.

The coin is stored compact (`StoredCoin`, 44 bytes: a seven-byte amount, scripts inline up to 32 bytes, P2WSH, P2TR and compressed P2PK as their 32 bytes; any other longer script on the heap), the flags share the word of the filed height, a map cell is 88 bytes, and nodes are linked by 32-bit numbers made of their 32 MiB region and their slot in it. A spent entry the store keeps (dirty, neither fresh nor owed: an erase the database is owed) lies in a spent cell of 44 bytes, the link, the key and the entry's word without the coin (`Entry::spent_cell`): the word comes before the coin for that, and the filed height has 24 bits. Spent cells come from a second node allocator whose numbers carry the top bit, so that one chain links both kinds; each allocator numbers 2^12 regions (128 GiB of cells). A confirmed spend that keeps its entry moves it into a spent cell and a coin written over one moves it back (`ShrinkDetached()`, `GrowDetached()`), between `Detach()` and `Attach()` and never while a run is in flight; a spent cell counts 44 bytes less in the tier's usage and in its span's sum. Mainnet at `-dbcache=2000` 1.141x faster than the store without them, level at `-dbcache=30000` (7.1, 24:13.54 and 10:04.39); unit case `spent_entries_lie_in_spent_cells`. An entry is on no list: it is counted in its shard's dirty count and the byte sum of its change height's span of 1,024 heights, or as clean (5.8). An entry is placed by the salted hash of its outpoint (`KeyHash()`), with a salt of the store's own, fixed where the store is made deterministic as master's cache is. The hash is the salted SipHash of master and is not to be touched (the maintainer, 24 November). Eviction sweeps each shard's map as a clock, a clean entry looked up since the last sweep keeping its place once.

Shards. 256 (`CoinsStore::DEFAULT_SHARDS`, measured in 4.9), picked by key range (4.8), each with a plain `Mutex`. Byte, entry and dirty totals are in per-thread stripes (`Totals`), so the publisher's size queries read no shard. Under a reindex the publisher is the thread `b-initload`.

Dirty bytes per shard are summed in a vector of spans indexed by `height >> 10` (`DIRTY_SPAN_BITS`); entries keep their exact height, and `BoundForBytes()` finds a run's bound from the span sums and, inside the span that holds it, from the heights of the entries of the shards that have entries there (unit case `bounds_are_exact_inside_and_across_dirty_spans`). A write-out run walks the shard's map for the dirty entries at or below its bound, starting over at each chunk. A tree per shard keyed by height costs a node per dirty coin; with spans, memory per output on the maintainer's run was 147 B against 207 B (measured when the spans held lists).

The view. `BlockApplyView` is what a block is connected into on top of the store: a `CoinsViewOverlay` with its prefetch and the block's own changes, which reads through the store and leaves nothing there while the block is validated, so a block that fails leaves the store as it was. A block connected alone is committed with `CommitAlone(block, undo)`, which hands its changes to `ApplyConfirmed()`; a spend whose coin the store does not hold, because the view read it from the database without keeping it, is made with the coin the block's undo data has for it. `ConfirmCommitted()` moves the store's best block and resets the view. Whether a coin read from the database stays in the store as a clean entry is the caller's choice (`SetKeepBaseReads()`): a window block keeps them, a block connected alone does not, so that a block at the tip that fails leaves none of its inputs in the cache, as on master. A view whose block is committed by another thread hands over its changes as items of sixteen bytes (`CommitItem`: a spend by the input's place in the block, a create by its transaction and output number, the coin made from the block by the commit) and is emptied with `ChangesCommitted()`; a block connected alone empties its view the same way at the end of `CommitAlone()` (the map cleared, its pool made again and reserved, 5.2a).

Judged by reading, nothing measured:

- The copy of a run's coins into its chunk stays: serialising straight from the entry would hold the shard through the serialisation. A `Coin`'s script holds up to 36 bytes inline (the stored entry 32, and P2WSH, P2TR and compressed P2PK outputs as their 32 bytes), so the copy is about a hundred bytes and no allocation for every common output type.
- Scripts above the assumevalid point: a windowed block runs its scripts inline on its one worker, so with `-assumevalid=0` the whole chain runs on the window's threads where master uses `-par`. Never measured as a comparison (the one such run is in 7.1).
- The kernel library does not link `common/system.cpp`; `CoinsStore::Flush` reads `std::thread::hardware_concurrency()` itself.

### 4.3 Memory made on one thread and freed on another: the cell pools, blocks

The cell pools. A store owns the memory of its cells: two pools (`CellPool`, cells and spent cells), each carving regions of 32 MiB (`NODE_REGION_BYTES`, `HintedRegion()`, with the huge page hint) in order into chunks of 16 KiB, numbering the cells by store, and giving the regions back when the store is destroyed. A shard's map takes a chunk at a time and gives out cells itself, under the shard's mutex, which every insertion and erase holds: the cells it frees go on a list of its own, linked by their first word, and serve its next insertions, the next one asked for ahead of its taking. So no memory changes threads, nothing is process-wide, and the only other lock is the pool's, once a chunk. Every number a map needs is at hand (an insertion's from the pool or its list, a chain walk's from its links), so no address is turned into a number and a region needs no alignment beyond the huge page's. The unaccounted part is the untouched tails of the shards' chunks (about 4 MiB over 256 shards and two pools) and the freed cells a shard keeps; the shards are even (keyed by the transaction id), which the pool per shard of upstream's `PoolResource` (7.3), taking chunks of its own at 1.55x the live nodes, was not taken to be. A pool's mutex and carving are a non-template `PoolRegions` under the typed `CellPool<T, KIND>`: clang's lock analysis does not see a negative requirement of a member of a class template nested in a class. A region is an aligned `operator new` of 32 MiB (`HintedRegion()`), which keeps up to 2 MiB of address space beside it (glibc serves the alignment from a larger mapping), never touched: no resident memory, but committed where commit is charged (Windows, Linux with strict overcommit), at most a sixteenth of the regions. Against the per-thread lists and process-wide depot before it (8.1): harness 0.995 to 1.008x, peak memory 8 to 11 MiB more at 450 and the floor; mainnet 9:45.53 against 9:40.25 (7.1). Memory measured against the series before them, such as the yardstick of 7.6 (+78 MiB over master at four threads and the floor), is that much lower than the series now.

What the store keeps beyond a store (read 28 November, at cfe30be5ee). No memory: two stores share none of their cells', and a destroyed store gives all of it back. Left:
- `AccessCoin()`'s copy: one thread-local `Coin` for every store, so the reference it returns is good until the thread's next call on any store, not only on the same one; a caller holding a reference from one chainstate's store while it asks another's would lose it. It comes with the `CCoinsViewCache` base, whose interface returns a reference the store has no stable place for (5.9); only a value-returning interface removes it, a larger change than it saves, not counted. `BlockApplyView` does not share it: its `AccessCoin()` returns the input's coin of the block (`InputCoin()`) or an entry of its own map. Audited 28 November (every production caller of `AccessCoin()`, with which views can be a store at run time): no caller holds a store's reference across a second call, none reads two chainstates' tips at once; a latent hazard for a future caller, not a present one.
- The key hash's salt (`KeyHash()`): one function-local static for all stores and shards, immutable once made, placing entries and deciding no outcome. Per store it would be about ten lines (the hasher beside the pools the map points at), net about zero, and the store's `deterministic` would then reach the shards. Optional.
- The totals' stripe index (`MyTotals()`): a process-wide counter deals each thread a stripe; each store has its own stripes. No memory, no coupling.
- Scratch vectors of the block apply (`ResolveInputs()` and the functions beside it): thread-local, cleared at each call, so nothing carries between calls or stores; their capacity stays with the thread, bounded by the largest block, and goes with the connect pool's threads when the chainstate manager ends. Outside the store, the block bins' thread-local in `NewBlock()` is of the same kind.
Sufficient for the pull request; the per-store salt is the next step if a kernel review asks.

Blocks. A block goes back, when its last reference is dropped, to a bin of the thread that built it, which frees what came back at its next `NewBlock()` (`ChainstateManager::NewBlock()`: a thread-local bin, a mutex and a vector, shared with the deleters; a thread's bin frees the rest when the thread ends and no block of it is held). The scheduler, which drops a connected block's last reference in its BlockConnected callback, only hands a pointer back. A window worker hashes the transactions of the block it reads itself. Blocks that came back to a thread that builds no more (a receive thread once the download is over: in the download the receive threads build every block, `HandOffBlock()`; outside it the message handler parses a full block inline) stay until it builds again or ends, about a window's worth at most, up to about 0.3 GiB outside `-dbcache` for the node's life. So the receive pool stops once the download is over and none of its tasks is left (`PeerManagerImpl::StopReceivePoolAfterDownload()`, from the message handler's `SendMessages()`): its threads end, and each thread's bin goes with the last of its blocks (the deleters hold it). Outside the download the message handler parses a full block inline (compact blocks are not made by `NewBlock()`), and its bin holds at most the last such block until the next. The bins are measured with `-reindex-chainstate` only, where the connect workers build the blocks; a network sync with them is not measured. The bins of the first form (a map of bins by thread id, emptied at the help point and at the end of an activation outside the download) were cut on 26 November and came back in this form on 28 November (8.1). A pool runs a task and lets it go outside the pool's lock (`ThreadPool`, util/threadpool.h): what the task holds, a receive task's message and block say, is freed without the other workers waiting on the lock, and may submit to the same pool.

Freed off the path as well: the previous block's transactions at the tip go to the connect pool (`Submit()` in `NewPoWValidBlock()`, the commit "net processing: the previous block's transactions are freed off a new block's path").

### 4.4 The lagging database: marker, runs, eviction, the owed erase

Three correctness flaws, fixed, each with a test:

1. The marker on disk was one run behind the written head in memory, while the lag ceiling and the prune gate decided by the value in memory; a pruned node at the ceiling could crash needing blocks already unlinked. Fix: a run's last write rewrites the marker to (new head, settled head).
2. A coin created and spent inside the lag is erased without a trace; if a reorg disconnects the spend and restores the coin, and the next multi-batch run stops after its first batch, the replay rolls forward along the new chain and the coin is lost. Master cannot do this, since it elides only coins created after the last flushed head. Fix: `DisconnectTip()` collects the coins the disconnected block restores that were created at or below the written head, when the block is above the written head on its branch, and hands them to the store (`KeepWritten`). The next run writes them before anything else, alone and without a marker, and a forced flush writes them before it flushes; until then the marker on disk is the old one, whose replay rolls forward along the old branch and leaves the reorganisation to be made again from the undo data. No forced full write is made before the first disconnect of a reorg (it would be one write of the dirty tier per reorg under `cs_main`). `feature_dbcrash.py` passed (the maintainer's run, 2026-11-21).
3. A lookup that misses (`Lookup()`) reads the base with the shard unlocked; a spend committed, written as an erase and settled in between would bring back a coin that no longer exists. Fix: a settle counter per shard, compared across the unlocked read.

A final run that overlaps publication is wrong as such: it would declare the database consistent at a block while a coin its successor spent was never written. A run therefore blocks the publisher (`StartWriteOutRun`: nothing is confirmed into the tier meanwhile; the window's periodic flush between blocks defers the wait and takes it with `cs_main` released, `m_defer_write_out_wait`; every other caller waits where it stands), so starting runs earlier only moves the wait. Overlapping them was judged by reading: the simple rule (cap the settled height below any uncollected dirty entry changed during the run) would hold the written height almost still, since published blocks spend coins of every age.

Runs:

- The write-out target is taken from the total the trigger measures; an event that only evicted does not move the periodic timer.
- The target is one half of that total since 17 November (three quarters before, with no rationale on record). Measured on the maintainer's machine, a reindex to 400,000 at `-dbcache=1024`: against one half, three quarters 0.96x (one pair), a quarter 0.92x, a full write 0.87x; a full write writes 42% more changes than three quarters. The connector is stopped 23% of the span at one half and at three quarters alike. Figures in /work/parallel-validation/performance-suggestions.md.
- Byte sums per shard by span of heights (4.2), a run that walks the shard's map for the dirty entries at or below its bound, eviction as a clock, the collected mark, and the streamed run (no copy of the run, no second lookup). Invariant: at most one collected chunk outstanding in all, and a collected entry is never erased before its chunk settles.
- A streamed chunk fills across shards to `-dbbatchsize` by an estimate of each change's batch bytes (45 plus the script for a coin, 38 for an erase), so a chunk is one batch, sorted like a flush batch (4.8). Basis: upstream's #31645 (batch sizes from 4 to 256 MiB measured; 32 MiB the default; flushing 5 to 30% faster by storage; nothing gained past 256 MiB); no measurement of my own. A chunk of one shard had never reached `-dbbatchsize`, so the simulated crash of `-dbcrashratio` never fired and `feature_dbcrash.py` failed on its own check that nodes had crashed: the crash hook needs a full batch. `-dbcrashratio` also fires after a non-final call's last batch when that batch is over half the batch size.
- A run collects the shards (`CollectChunk()`): an entry changed above the run's bound stays for a later run, unless an erase is owed under it.
- An erase owed under a create above the run's bound (the block re-creates an outpoint whose spend the database does not have yet) is written by the run as that erase (`RunWritesOwed` in the entry machine), also where the create went over an unspent coin the database never had; the entry then owes its coin alone, at the coin's height, for a later run.
- After a disconnect the written head is not an ancestor of the head; a run started in that state is forced final, and after a disconnect below the written head every run is final until one has begun (4.11, D2). A flush that would prune files the written head holds back first gets a run bounded at the height pruning may reach.
- A run is one effect on the executor with its completion under `cs_main`: the restored coins of a reorg, the block-file sync, the block index batch (with the syncs it owes) and the stream. Failures latch (`FlushFailed`, `SyncFailed`, `m_undo_write_failed`, `m_index_batch_lost`) and a later run or flush is refused instead of writing a marker over missing data. `-test=write_out_every_block` makes a run after every block, for tests.

Found while building: the existing unit test `processnewblock_signals_ordering` gives one block object to ten threads; with a block's own checks outside `cs_main` the memo flags in `CBlock` raced under ThreadSanitizer. The three flags are atomic now (`BlockCheckedFlag`).

### 4.5 The window

The path of a window block. The connector stages it under `cs_main` (`StageConnectTip()`), a worker of the connect pool parses it and enters its transactions in the window's outputs (`RunConnectTask()`, `WindowOutputs`), and once every lower block in flight is entered (`OnWindowBlockIndexed()`) whichever worker is there validates it: `RunWindowValidation()` runs `ConnectBlock()` with the scripts inline, read only against the store, and an input the store does not have is the output of a lower block's transaction, found in the window's outputs. A valid block's changes become items that point into the block, grouped by shard range into 64 groups (`PrepareCommit()`, `COMMIT_GROUPS`). The connector commits the block in the hold that publishes it (`PublishConnectTip()`), together with the complete, valid blocks above it: 32 at most (`MAX_COMMIT_BLOCKS`), 8 with a cache near its limit, one where a write of the coins is due. The groups are applied by the connector and by workers that come by, also between two transactions of a block they validate (every 64 transactions; `RunCommitWork()`, `CoinsStore::ApplyConfirmed()`), and every block of the commit is published before the hold ends. One commit job runs at a time (`WindowSync::job`). The store is at the tip whenever `cs_main` is free, so no reader and no write of the coins meets a block that is not published.

The window's outputs. `WindowOutputs` (validation.cpp) implements `WindowCoinSource` (coinsstore.h), the interface a `BlockApplyView` asks ahead of the store (`SetOverlay()`): `FindCoin()` for one outpoint, `FindInputs()` for a block's inputs at once by position, `NamedIds()` for which transaction ids a lower block may have made. It is one table for all the window's blocks, in shards by transaction id, in which an item keeps its block's height. A block's own outputs are read there as well and are not added to its view (`SetOwnLimit()`, `ConnectBlockContext::implicit_creates`). A published block's items are taken out by the workers (`Sweep()`), never by the thread that publishes. The table's ring of block places is 256 wide, and `static_assert(RING >= MAX_CONNECT_WINDOW)` keeps it at least as wide as the widest window, which the model of 9.2 shows is load-bearing.

Conflicts. The window's outputs know what its blocks make, not what they spend: a block that spends a coin a lower block of the window spent is valid in the window, and its spend finds no coin in the commit. That block and the blocks of the commit above it are then taken out of the store again, as a disconnected block is, with their undo data (`TakeBackCommit()`); the blocks below stay and are published. A failure of a window block decides nothing, in its validation or in its commit: the step ends, the window drains, and the block is connected again alone, where its verdict is master's. A node error is not retried. Cost: an invalid block in the window is validated twice. Unit cases `invalid_window_block_is_judged_alone` and `double_spend_in_the_window_is_taken_back`; a take-back whose read of the undo data fails has no test.

No further conditions on when the window opens are to be proposed (the maintainer: "I don't like that we are introducing more exceptions."). The window rule is one expression: not a retry, the mode is download, the main-chain reorg lock is not held, and the window is wider than one.

Undo. A block's undo data is settled on the worker that validated it (`ConnectBlockResult::undo_size`, `SettleUndo`): measured if it is to be written, freed if not.

Size. The width when `-connectwindow` is 0 is twice the connect workers (`ConnectWindow()`), at most `MAX_CONNECT_WINDOW` 64. Above 32 threads the window is less than twice the pool; above 64 the further threads only take received blocks and the tip's prevout fetch. The byte cap, `ChainstateManager::m_window_bytes`'s limit, is `max(coins cache / WINDOW_BYTES_SHARE (8), MIN_WINDOW_BYTES (32 MiB))`; the eighth is a choice, not a measurement, the floor is about six late mainnet blocks. A staged block counts its transactions at 512 bytes each (`WINDOW_BYTES_PER_ITEM`) until it is parsed; then it counts (transactions + inputs) * 512 but at least the parsed block's size, which the worker estimates from the serialized size and the counts of transactions, inputs and outputs (`ParsedBlockBytes()`) and does not measure. The window's cap reads the configured coins cache size, never its usage, so a full `-dbcache` does not narrow the window. The cap is the intended memory bound for small machines.

Exceptions on the pool, on the maintainer's word ("if it is not a std::runtime_error inherited exception, then just terminate"): the connect task, a block's validation and the write-out run go through `util::RunWorkerTask` (util/worker_task.h), which catches std::runtime_error alone, and the pool functions that call it are noexcept; the receive task catches `std::ios_base::failure` alone (4.7). Consequence: a std::logic_error on a pool worker terminates the process, since the pool would keep it in a future nobody reads and the connector would wait for it for ever.

Bench lines. One `Connect window: ...` line is logged at shutdown (blocks validated on the pool, the width, conflicts, blocks retried alone), and the connector's lines.

Form. A staged block's phase is a type (`connect_phase::Staged`, `ReadFailed`, `Validated`) and its place in the window a state of `node::WindowBlockState` (Running, then Unreadable, or Indexed, Validating, Finished) that changes through `WINDOW_BLOCK_TABLE` (node/connect_machine.h) under the window's mutex; the connector's waits read it through `IsIndexed()`, `IsComplete()` and `IsValidating()`. The connect step is planned and finished in separate functions (`PlanConnectStep` and `FinishConnectStep`, over a `ConnectStep` struct). A block connected alone takes the same three parts and commits with `CommitAlone()` (4.2). The connector waits between blocks, without `cs_main`, on the undo-backlog and pinned-blocks accounts as it does on the window's.

Measured (the harness, chain DX `-reindex-chainstate`, 16 workers, 24 November, the tree of the tip): 580.4 blocks/s at `-dbcache=2000` and 494.3 at 450; the series at cecbce8958, whose window applied blocks to the store while they were validated, gave 564.8 and 499.5 in the same rounds. A second pair of rounds the same evening, with the bench binary rebuilt at 525940e906 (two runs each, about four seconds a run, load average rising from 5 to 10 through the rounds): 630.4 and 642.3 at 2000 and 549.3 and 550.2 at 450, against 580.4 and 555.8, and 445.2 and 443.9; the UTXO hash as expected. Why these stand further apart than the first rounds was not looked into (H). At the tip 62b2b93400 (25 November, `build-bench`, foreign load about one core): the UTXO hash as expected (246,143 txouts, `d4ba681a…dc59`, no window conflict), and 676.8 blocks/s at `-dbcache=2000` (675.8, 677.8) and 588.1 at 450 (587.9, 588.4); not run against an earlier binary in the same rounds, so how much of the difference to the readings above is the quieter host is not known. The maintainer's mainnet run is in 7.1 (10:36.16 against 10:32.54 for cecbce8958). The measurements made while the design was built are in 7.13.

Inputs by position. Before a window block is checked, its inputs are resolved into a list by their position in the block: from the window's outputs (`WindowCoinSource::FindInputs()`, which also answers an output of one of the block's earlier transactions), the tier (`CoinsStore::FetchInto()`) or the database. `ConnectBlock()` hands the view each transaction and its part of the list before checking it (`BlockValidationContext::inputs_for`), and the view answers `AccessCoin()` and `HaveCoin()` for an input's own `prevout`, which it finds by the address, from the list; the checks are master's and read through the view. The undo data is taken from the list, and the view's map is not filled. That map was also what failed a second spend of an outpoint inside a block, and the commit cannot take its place: an output an earlier transaction of the block makes never becomes a commit item. So the block keeps the outpoints it has spent in a table of its own, asked before each transaction is checked (`feature_connect_window_double_spend.py`).

### 4.6 BIP30 in the window, the stretch below BIP34

Whether a block is checked for BIP30 is decided from the header chain when its context is built (`EnforceBIP30()`, `ConnectBlockContext::enforce_bip30`), and every block but the genesis block takes the window, checked or not: the window opens from the start. On mainnet the check is on below BIP34 and from height 1,983,702; on chains whose BIP34 hash never matches (regtest, signet, testnet4) always, so regtest connect figures need the check off: `-test=bip30_off` (`CChainParams::Bip30Off()`).

The check asks for all of a block's outputs at once (`CCoinsViewCache::HaveAnyOutput()`, which the view overrides): the window's outputs are asked once for each transaction (`NamedIds()`), the store once for each shard (`CoinsStore::MayHave()`), and only an output one of them may have is looked up as the cache looks it up. A window block therefore sees the outputs of the lower blocks in flight and the store's coins. What it cannot see is that a lower block in flight spent such a coin; a block that fails the check for that reason fails in the window, which is no verdict (4.5), and is connected again alone.

The two overwrite blocks (91,842 over 91,812; 91,880 over 91,722) are not checked (`IsBIP30Repeat()`), and their coinbase goes over the earlier coin: `ApplyConfirmed()` makes the create, and its report keeps the coin the create went over, which a take-back needs. Where only the database holds that coin, the store reports the create as one that may overwrite and found no entry (`ConfirmedReport`), and the take-back puts back the database's coin (`CoinsStore::BaseCoin()`), which nothing writes in the hold; that path has no test. Over a confirmed coin the database does not have yet, the erase is owed under the new coin (`CoinOverErase`) and a run writes it (`RunWritesOwed`). Unit cases: the erase an entry owes across a create, a disconnect and a spend (coinsstore_tests), and a coinbase that goes over a confirmed coin through the view. No test drives a duplicate outpoint through the real window end to end.

### 4.7 The receive side

Goal and rules. The maintainer paused the connect work at the 17:39 run ("17 minutes is good") and set the next subject, the network side of a sync: a local simulation; a generated regtest chain of full-size blocks; served by a bunch of nodes on unmodified master; only the receiving side may change; no signet. The goal: twice master's speed with heavy blocks, master as the client against the branch as the client on the same chain. Stated limit: where master already takes all the links deliver, no receive-side change can double it. A mainnet snapshot route was talked through and not taken (no clean way to fetch one here).

The receive pool. `PeerManagerImpl::HandOffBlock` decides from the 80 header bytes: in initial block download (never with `Options::blocks_on_message_handler`, which unit and fuzz tests set), with the header's hash in `mapBlocksInFlight` for this peer, the message's bytes go to a task of the receive pool, `PeerManagerImpl::m_receive_pool`: `RECEIVE_THREADS` = 5 threads of its own, enough for a 2.5 Gbit/s line where a thread deserialises and checks about 63 MB of heavy blocks a second, or none where the connect pool has fewer than two workers. Handler and task share `ProcessBlockMessage`: deserialise, mutation check, bookkeeping, the three-hold acceptance, the write, the block cache, the connector's signal, the same functions in the same order as master's handler. The task hashes the transactions itself and leaves the activation to the connector. `RunBlockTask` is `noexcept` and catches only `std::ios_base::failure`. Shutdown: `PeerManager::FinishBlockTasks()` after `connman->Stop()` and before `StopConnector()`. Test: `p2p_ibd_block_handoff.py`.

- Peer rules: a task sets the flag and wakes the handler; the handler checks `m_should_discourage` before the peer's next message and `SendMessages` disconnects first thing; a task drops its block when the peer is gone or marked, and the drop removes the request so the block is asked for again. So at most the pool's width of further blocks from a bad peer are processed, of at most 16 in transit. Bytes that are no block, on the pool, remove the request and disconnect the peer.
- Why a pool of its own: `Chainstate::DrainWindow` waits for the connect pool's window tasks with `cs_main` held, and a receive task takes `cs_main`; on one pool, every worker on a receive task would leave both waiting for ever. On the receive pool no task the connector waits for queues behind one that waits for `cs_main`. A connect pool of one worker gives the receive pool no thread, which is why the functional suite (`prevoutfetchthreads=1`) sees every block on the handler. At most one task for each receive thread is outstanding; the handler waits beyond that, so memory outside `-dbcache` is at most five messages and their blocks.
- A block on the pool stays in flight until it is stored; without that the freed handler requested the same blocks again (4130 requests for 2449 blocks). But it does not keep one of the peer's sixteen places: it is flagged (`QueuedBlock::with_pool`) and `SendMessages` counts only the unflagged requests against the sixteen, so the pass that follows the block message asks for the next block. A peer can have sixteen blocks on the way plus the pool's task limit.
- A task always wakes the handler when done (at 24 workers 448 to 507 blocks/s against 371 to 405 without); the stalling timeout is cleared at the hand-off; a second copy of a block whose first is with its task is dropped; the task set is (peer, hash).
- Read and found sound: `NotifyHeaderTip` can deliver two header-tip notifications out of order (cosmetic, already possible against an RPC thread); `FlushStateToDisk(NONE)` in `FinishAcceptBlock` does work only on a pruned node, under `cs_main`, and waits for the effects executor (since the IO-4 fix of 5.1 it is skipped while a step that uses the window runs); block-file order shifts by at most the task limit, pruning's bookkeeping is per file; `FinalizeNode` can run beside a task, which holds no connection.
- The hand-off is a machine (the handoff states), the initial block download latch is read once per message and handed down (`in_ibd`), transaction acceptance waits for blocks still with a task (`WaitForPendingBlocks`), the stall verdict on a peer is deferred while tasks are outstanding, and `ProcessGetBlockData` keeps a request while the block is being stored.
- The hash pool was removed: with blocks hashed in their tasks it served only blocks that stay on the handler and the block-file threads of a reindex, and neither it nor the block helper `DeserializeBlock()` showed outside the spread (7.7). `ProcessBlockMessage`, `ReadBlock` and `LoadExternalBlockFile` deserialise with the plain operator over a `SpanReader`, which hashes from the bytes; the loader reads a block's bytes into a buffer first for that. Were the loader ever the bound of a full reindex, its serial rate without the pool is about 0.83x (loader CPU 33 s against 27.5 s). Alternatives weighed in the plan and not taken: transactions built on hash threads (strictly immediate disconnect, moves only deserialisation), one lane per peer (nothing for one peer), several message handler threads (outside this branch).

`-test=bip30_off` sets `CChainParams::Bip30Off()`, read by `EnforceBIP30()`, and BIP34 keeps regtest's height 1, so a full `-reindex` accepts the genesis block again (a first form set `BIP34Height = 0`; the genesis block then failed BIP34 on its version and its coinbase, and with no valid block `InvalidChainFound()` aborted on `assert(tip)`). `feature_connect_window.py` reindexes the blocks after the chainstate in both variants.

The kernel's block cache is 128 MiB by default (`DEFAULT_BLOCK_CACHE_BYTES`), not clamped to the coins cache as bitcoind's is; a library user sets it with `btck_chainstate_manager_options_set_block_cache_bytes()`, zero disabling it as `-blockcache=0` does for bitcoind (documented at `ChainstateManagerOpts::block_cache_bytes`). The `-par` help says the window's scripts are not bounded by it.

A requested block that does not deserialise: on the receive pool the request is dropped for the block to be asked for again and the peer is disconnected whatever its permissions, so that it is not asked again to answer the same way; on the message handler master's handling stays, the exception logged and the peer kept until the download timeout. Making them agree would change master's policy for every block message; the comment at `RunBlockTask` gives the reason for the disconnect: the request no longer counts, so no timeout would end it, and a kept peer would be asked again without end.

The request rule's test, `p2p_ibd_request_rule.py`, drives a peer's delivery times with mocktime, which both clocks of the rule follow (`NodeClock::now()` at receive, the request time in `SendMessages`): one block until the first arrived, one at a time at four seconds a block, then a count that starts at one, never falls and reaches sixteen within twenty deliveries as it delivers at once, and back to one after a round at four seconds a block. It fails on master (16 asked for at once). With one block outstanding the sample would be the round trip, which held a far peer at one block; the peer's least ping is taken off the time of a block that started at its request, so it is not (from the outside review of 25 November, 5.2a). Also known: the rule limits how many blocks a slow peer holds, not which, so it can still hold the lowest missing block.

Blocks at the tip go to the connector too: `ProcessBlock` activates asynchronously whenever `Options::blocks_on_message_handler` is off, and `SignalConnector(block)` leaves the block just accepted for the connector's next `ActivateBestChain()` as the block in hand, so it is connected from memory and not read back. Test `p2p_tip_connector.py` (the tip's `UpdateTip` on the `connect` thread and a "Using cached block" line; fails when the connector is given no block). Measured with a throwaway harness (`tipbench/tip_latency.py`: a block of 2000 unseen transactions from one test peer, getheaders timed in a loop from another), four rounds each: the longest answer 110 to 117 ms before, 104 to 108 ms after, the tip in 143 to 151 ms either way; an older master binary (4519933391) 110.6 ms with the same 99 ms "Verify 2000 txins". So no cost and no gain: the handler takes the next message, but getheaders, getdata and nearly every other message need `cs_main`, which the connection holds through script verification as on master. A gain at the tip needs the scripts checked before `cs_main` is taken; not attempted.

The per-peer request rule (31 lines in `net_processing.cpp`). In initial block download a peer has outstanding at most one block more than it delivers in two seconds, going by a smoothed time between its block arrivals (weight 1/4; the sample is taken whenever `now >= since`, with a floor of one microsecond), never more than sixteen, and one until its first block arrived. Outside initial block download nothing changes. No message changes; a peer sees fewer hashes in a getdata.

### 4.8 The flush

Why master's shape stalls. The coins database's write buffer is 2 MiB (8 MiB cache / 4, `dbwrapper.cpp`), so every 32 MiB batch is its own memtable and level-0 file; 12 level-0 files stop writes (`kL0_StopWritesTrigger`) until the one background thread has compacted them. With shards picked by salted hash every file spanned the whole key space and each compaction rewrote level 1: one batch in 12.2 stalled. Also `WriteRun` put the marker keys (`DB_BEST_BLOCK` B and `DB_HEAD_BLOCKS` H, which bracket the coin keys C) into every batch, so every batch spanned all coin keys.

What is built:

1. `CCoinsViewDB` remembers the marker it left open (`m_open_marker` under `m_marker_mutex`): the marker is opened once in the first batch and closed by the final one; a `WriteRun` naming the same pair of heads writes coins only; `BatchWrite` and a final run forget it.
2. `ShardIndex` is `(ReadBE32(txid) * shards) >> 32`, the leading four bytes of the txid as the key serialises it, monotonic for any shard count; the in-shard map keeps its salted hash. Side effects: a transaction's outputs share a shard, and the shard pick is grindable (contention on one shard and a larger share of a forced flush's scratch memory, not complexity).
3. A store batch is whole neighbouring shards and ends between two shards only, at most 32 of them (`MAX_BATCH_SHARDS`, 9.2; the limit keeps the held shard mutexes under ThreadSanitizer's 64); a shard larger than a batch is a run of its own, which `CCoinsViewDB::WriteRun()` cuts at `-dbbatchsize` along the keys. `-dbbatchsize` stays the bound on what the database is given at once; what grows is what a worker holds before it writes, a whole shard's changes (a 256th of the tier), moved out of the map, not a second copy. Each shard's changes are sorted into descending key order (`SortWrites`), walked from the highest shard down, because H sorts above the coins. `SortWrites` orders the outputs of one transaction by the outpoint, which is the key order except past the 16511th output (the key's VARINT of the index grows from two bytes to three and sorts lower bytewise); an overlap there is two pieces touching in one transaction, placed a level higher instead of merged.
4. `MAX_WIPE_WORKERS{4}`: a worker for every two batches of dirty set, capped by cores and shards, each with a contiguous range of shards; the one with the highest range runs on the calling thread, writes the marker's batch, and starts the others once it is in the database; the others are tasks of the connect pool, and the caller takes the ranges no worker claimed. A default cache of 450 MiB flushes on one thread; never more workers than cores.
5. The order of a forced flush is the table `FLUSH_TABLE` (`FlushPhase`, node/durability_machine.h). The final sync of a completed undo file runs inside the undo write's own effect (`FinishUndoFile`); where the block file was left only after the claim, it is queued as an effect right behind the write.

Why it works: LevelDB places a non-overlapping memtable down to level 2 (`PickLevelForMemTableOutput`) and moves files without rewriting (`IsTrivialMove`); read, not run. A cut in the middle of a shard's walk had defeated it for a shard of more than half a batch (any flush above about 4 GiB at 256 shards): the walk is in the map's hash order and each piece was sorted after the cut, so the two pieces were two batches over the same key range, batches overlapped in a chain down the shards, and the database fell behind and stopped its writers. Hence item 3, not more shards: the maintainer's objection ("Doesn't more shards = more baseline memory, which is not compatible with the low-power setups restriction?") stands unanswered by measurement (only the harness's level peak resident memory from 16 to 4096 shards on a short chain; the per-shard footprint on a long chain, the dirty spans in particular, was never read or measured), and the stalls were a large-cache condition, so raising the default would charge every setup for it.

Upstream: #31875 sorted batches (a 26 GiB dump from 21:56 to 8:27), closed by its author when later benchmarks cast doubt; sipa's objection was the sort's memory at the peak, which sorting a shard at a time does not have.

Unit cases: `flush_batches_are_disjoint_key_ranges`, `flush_never_ends_a_batch_inside_a_shard`; `flush_fills_batches_across_shards` changed minimally (it had counted on the fill inside a shard). Not exercised: a crash in the middle of a flush or between lag runs under the remembered marker; a flush interrupted between the batches of four workers. Runs and probes in 7.1 and 7.2.

### 4.9 Defaults

Measured on the regtest heavy chain (`tune/` harness: `-reindex-chainstate` or a sync from one local peer, pinned with `taskset`; `hardware_concurrency()` does not shrink under `taskset`, so a small machine is emulated by pinning and passing the `-par` it would derive; single runs repeat within 3 to 5%).

| Value | Now | Measured |
|---|---|---|
| Connect pool threads | `GetNumCores()` clamped to [2, `MAX_PREVOUTFETCH_THREADS` 1024] in `node::DefaultPrevoutFetchThreads()` | the rate follows min(workers, cores) on a grid of 1 to 16 cores and workers at dbcache 450; threads beyond the cores add memory (2-core sync resident 128 to 189 MiB from two to eight workers) and no rate. The floor of two: one worker keeps a peer's blocks on the handler, 1.17x slower on one core |
| Window width at `-connectwindow=0` | twice the connect workers (was min(workers, cores)) | reindex 1.14x at two cores, 1.17x at four, 1.2 to 1.3x at sixteen; sync level |
| Window byte floor | 32 MiB under the cache/8 (4.5) | at an 8 MiB cache reindex 88 to 180 blocks/s at two cores, 90 to 585 at sixteen; no change from 256 MiB up. The whole small-cache loss was the budget falling below one block, which narrows the window to a single block; the write-out logic under it cost nothing measurable |
| Shards | 256 (was 1024) | 64 loses 8 to 10%; 256, 1024 and 4096 equal at 8 to 16 workers and under write-out pressure |
| Block cache | the coins cache size clamped to [32 MiB, 128 MiB] when `-blockcache` is unset (`node::DefaultBlockCacheBytes()`); 128 MiB from the default `-dbcache` up | at `-dbcache=30` 32 MiB gives 0.81x the resident memory at an equal rate (pruned sync, 8 workers); a default of an eighth of the cache cost 0.96x at `-dbcache=450`, so the default follows the coins cache size, held between 32 MiB and 128 MiB (7.7) |

The maximum of 1024 threads is the maintainer's word (from 32). The kernel's `DEFAULT_PREVOUTFETCH_THREADS` stays 8 for library users, unit tests and fuzz; the functional framework still writes `prevoutfetchthreads=1`. Nothing above 16 cores was measured here; 64 and 1024 threads run correctly on 16 cores with 344 to 436 MiB resident against 218 to 290.

Options the series adds or changes: `-prevoutfetchthreads` (default as in the table, the floor of two being `MIN_DEFAULT_PREVOUTFETCH_THREADS`; 0 for no pool), `-connectwindow` (debug only; 0 for twice the workers, 1 for no window), `-blockcache`, the `-par` help text, and for tests `-test=bip30_off`, `-test=write_out_every_block` and `-test=crash_before_undo_write`.

Corners at the defaults of the day (8 workers then), heavy-section blocks/s, master / branch: reindex at 2 cores 68-78 / 180; at 4 cores 72 / 340; at 16 cores 70 / 600, the same at an 8 MiB and a 450 MiB cache. Sync from one peer: 73 / 140 at 2 cores, 64 / 225 at 4, 61 / 172-185 at 12. Resident memory at harness scale: 181 MiB against master's 150 at sixteen cores, 158 against 121 at two.

What the values do not do:

- Memory outside `-dbcache`: the window's blocks (at least 32 MiB), the block cache (32 to 128 MiB; 128 MiB by default in the kernel library, which a library user may set, 4.7), the undo backlog (up to 64 MiB) and the pinned blocks are not in the coins cache budget; each has a count of its own (`util::ByteAccount`, or the executor's backlog). The default block cache is the coins cache size held between 32 MiB and 128 MiB (an explicit `-blockcache` overrides it); one counted byte budget for all parsed blocks was designed and not built, because on the harness memory follows the block cache size and the rate does not (7.7).
- Idle connect workers cost CPU on a sync from one peer: two workers and a window of four gave the same rate as eight and sixteen for 0.75x the CPU. Cause not read.
- Empty blocks at two cores: 12,500 to 13,300 a second against master's 16,500 (0.78x); at sixteen cores 19,000 to 23,000 against 15,200. Where it goes (pass 3 tip, `empty/` harness: 40,000 coinbase-only blocks, `-reindex-chainstate`, `-par=2`, two connect workers, per-thread figures from `/proc` between heights 4000 and 38000):
  - Master is one thread. `initload` spends 59 to 60 us of CPU on a block, of 61 us of wall; the scheduler thread (the validation signals) 19 to 21 us beside it. 80 us in all, 16,200 to 16,600 blocks/s over four runs.
  - The branch spends more CPU on the same block and spreads it: `initload` 30 to 37 us, the two workers 22 to 28 us each, the effects thread 6 us, the scheduler 16 to 19 us; 94 to 118 us in all. Reads, writes and page faults per block equal master's (two reads, one write, the log line), so none of it is I/O.
  - On two cores five threads share what master gives to one and a third: they stand runnable without a core for 91 to 115 us a block, the scheduler thread the longest. Six runs gave 12,600 to 15,700 blocks/s, 0.78x to 0.96x of master, where master's runs differ by 2%.
  - The same binary and settings on four cores: 17,700 (1.09x master), on eight 18,300 (1.12x), with the waiting gone (single runs).
  - Fewer workers do not help: with one worker 12,000 to 12,400, with none 11,300 to 11,800 (0.70x), the same at two and at eight cores. With no worker the branch's own thread spends 81 us on a block where master's spends 59 (1.37x), and the effects thread 8 us more. So the base cost of a block is higher, and the workers hide it only where cores are free.
  - Which code holds the extra 22 to 30 us is not established: no profiler runs on this box. The stage timers (`-debug=bench`, both slowed by the logging, no workers) put about 19 us of it in the connect step up to its end and about 9 us in `Confirm` where master has `Flush`; the stage labels of the branch are taken from timestamps of an earlier stage and say no more than that.

### 4.10 Pruning

Block files: `BlockManager::PinBlockFile()`/`UnpinBlockFile()`, both prune finders skip a file with a write in flight; the prune order of 4.11 (D1, D3).

The prune rule. Pruning never passes the coins database's written head, and nothing else limits a pruning node's lag: the fixed ceiling (`LagCeiling()`, 256 in prune mode, a run about every 129 blocks at any cache size) is removed.

- The limit. `FlushStateToDisk` caps the height both finders may prune to at the written head; when the written head is not on the active chain the cap is the fork point with the active chain, so the left branch's blocks above the fork stay too. A file is unlinked only once a block index that records it is on disk (D1 of 4.11), and that index is written after the coins the limit was computed from are on disk, so at no instant does the durable index call a block pruned that a replay from the durable coins database needs.
- Bringing the database forward. When pruning would take files, a write-out run to the prune height is started before the finders choose, and settled before they run. Two conditions gate it. Pruning would take files: a manual request is pending, or `BlockManager::AtPruneTarget()` holds, which repeats the conditions of `FindFilesToPrune()` (the chain above `PruneAfterHeight`, the block files plus one chunk of each kind at the target). And the written head holds a whole file back: `BlockManager::HasBlockFileEndingWithin()` finds a block file ending between the written head and the prune height. The first form, a run at every prune check, fell from 120 to 43 blocks/s; the second, the file condition alone, made a run for each block file from the start of a sync, also while the files were far below the target and nothing could be pruned (found by the review of 4.11 and by the reimplementation, whose gate counts the held-back bytes against the target; the predicate here is the coarser form of the same rule). A node that prunes only on request writes nothing without one. The disk space is checked before the run as before a flush (`CheckDiskSpace`, "Disk space is too low!"), taken from the reimplementation. A run is refused with an error once a flush or a run failed (`FlushFailed()`), where it had reached an `Assert`.
- In the window: automatic pruning does not wait for the run where it stands (`prune_deferred`); inside the publishing hold the flag `m_prune_at_periodic_write` leaves automatic pruning to that hold's periodic write, which starts the run without waiting for it (`prune_deferred`), and the files are chosen at the following flush. The executor is settled only when pruning would take files (a request, or the block files at the prune target), not at every prune check, and a failed run unlinks nothing.
- What it bought (H, 7.7): a pruned sync at `-dbcache=450` at the same rate (121.1 against 120.4 blocks/s) with 0.55x the coins written and 2,269 erases against 232,428; 23 database commits where the ceiling had asked for a flush 1016 times. At `-dbcache=30` the cache drives the runs and nothing changes (37.2 against 38.1).
- The two gaps the ceiling had hidden, both closed: the run's effect committed the block index, with the chosen files already flagged as pruned, before it streamed the coins, so a crash inside the stream left a written head below blocks the index called gone (master has the same window between its index write and its coins flush); and the gate compared heights, not branches, so after a reorganisation deeper than 32 blocks a file holding blocks of the left branch could go while a replay still needed their undo data.
- The benchcoin cost that led here (7.5): `StreamWriteOut` 330 to 395 s of CPU, the database's background thread 1340 s where near-master's has 279 s, 390 GB written for 102 GB received, all with the ceiling. No real-network run without it exists.
- Test: `feature_coins_lag_prune.py`, a crash inside the coins write of a manual prune (the existing `-dbcrashratio=1 -dbbatchsize=300`), then a kill right after files were deleted; each restart rolls forward to the same tip and UTXO hash as an unpruned peer. Never exercised by any test: automatic pruning together with the lag, a written head off the active chain, a crash between the index write and the unlink, a snapshot chainstate.

### 4.11 The review of the series and its fixes

The series was reviewed by reading in twelve rounds from 13 to 15 November, four to nine readers each, mostly by area. Every verdict before the fixes was not ACK; the confirmed defects were fixed in the commits that introduced them. The full record of each round (the findings by letter, the fixes, what was left and why, the maintainer's rulings, the backup branches of the time) is `/work/parallel-validation/first-cut-review-record.md`, moved there word for word from this section, from 4.7 and from 5.3 on 2026-11-18; a finding cited by letter elsewhere in this document (D1, D2, V3 and the like) is in that file. A second review followed the restatement as state machines, in twelve rounds of its own: `/work/parallel-validation/review-plan-full-series.md` with the `full-rN-verified.md` files beside it.

What each of those rounds changed in the design:

| Round | Date, at | What it changed |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 13 Nov, the 43 commits | a create over a visible coin is a conflict and the block is retried alone, which gives master's verdict; a window block where BIP30 is enforced creates with `must_be_new`, and a spend carries the coin it read; failed undo writes and flushes latch and stop every chainstate write; pruning settles the run in flight, unlinks a file only once an index that records it is on disk, and syncs block and undo data first (4.10); `m_fell_below_written` (4.4) |
| 2 (pass 3) | 14 Nov, 4f3d627536 | a failed run ends all writes (`AbandonWriteOut()`); `invalidateblock` settles undo writes and their completions first; outside initial block download `PublishConnectTip()` waits for the effects executor; no prune-gate run for each block file (4.10) |
| 3 | 14 Nov, 3e2a414940 | a deferred transaction over an unspent coin of its own txid fails the block, which is retried alone (a crafted block reached `AddCoin`'s logic_error); a restored coin keeps its creation height (`TagFor()`) |
| 4 | 14 Nov, daaa0d893b | the repeat guard for every non-coinbase transaction of a block that may defer; `CheckBlock()` before the header, as on master; a reorganisation announces the re-added transactions before the connected blocks; a second copy of a block keeps the first sender's record; then a disconnect writes the restored coins the database never held, in place of a forced write before every reorganisation |
| 5 | 14 Nov, 0678d96583 | that write only when the written head is an ancestor, and carried by the next run (`KeepWritten()`); the coins database's cache no longer resized (5.6); no write-out check inside a reorganisation step; the tip's notification hold one with its publication; `-test=bip30_off` and the window test over it; then the snapshot's written head set at activation |
| 6 | 14 Nov, 35adc2d84b | the uncache tracepoint; one `CheckBlockTransaction()` for both paths |

The ralph review of 26 November (willcl-ark's runs 3173 to 3182, on a split of an older head; triage in `/work/parallel-validation/ralph-triage-2026-11-27-{A,B,C}.md`, plan in `ralph-plan-2026-11-27.md`), fixed on the night of 27 to 28 November, each fix in the commit that introduced the code, tip 08043e77b2:

Dropped first as not holding (a run writes the head's value), the finding was confirmed by the extended LagRuns model: a run bounded below the coin settles below its creation with the coin written, and after a reorganisation back below it the next final run's marker opens with equal heads before the coin's erase lands. The unit test `restore_over_an_owed_erase_keeps_it_owed` fails without the fix.
