Slab fragmentation simulated (26 November, on the maintainer's word "simulate first"; `/work/utxo-partial-order-harness/mainnet/slabsim.py`): an outside reviewer's (Neon's) age-ordered variable-record store, one shard at 27 records a block (about 7,000 outputs a block over 256 shards), 100,000 blocks, the full-cache regime (no coins read from the database, no tombstones), a coin spent in its own block never stored; a sealed slab under its threshold has its survivors copied into the shard's old slab. Spend ages both as recorded in 7.5 and as mainnet.observer's. Waste (slab memory over live records) and copies per stored coin, alike for both distributions within 1 to 4 points: 64 KiB slabs at 0.85 13% at 3.0 to 3.3 copies, at 0.95 4% at 8.7 to 9.7; 256 KiB at 0.85 13% at 1.9 to 2.2, at 0.70 29% at 1.0 to 1.2; 1 MiB at 0.85 23 to 24% at 1.2 to 1.4. The young slab's threshold does not matter (a young slab is below any of them when it is sealed: most of its coins die while it fills); the old slabs, which lose long-lived coins slowly and are copied again, set the waste. With 13 to 17% waste a record of 69 bytes (aligned) and a 4.7-byte index slot make 82 to 86 bytes a coin, the node 16.2 to 16.9 GiB at mainnet's peak with the 1.83 GiB beside the cache, against 20.0 for the store of that day. On paper beside it, fixed cells without list links (runs and eviction scanning the shard, a height and flag word kept) 95 bytes (18.6 GiB), with a 4-byte index in place of the chain link 90 bytes (17.7 GiB), no records moved.

The window traced (26 November, the maintainer's run with `-debug=window` from a build with per-block window timestamps, `fbd423d26abb`, `-dbcache=30000`, the 9:49 command; log `/work/run32_window.log`, read with `windowstats.py` and `commitgroups.py` in `/work/utxo-partial-order-harness/mainnet`; 9:39.81 in all, the last block 540.6 s after its start line, shutdown 39.0 s). The window (60 wide, 30 workers) holds 57 to 59 blocks from 150,000 on: always full. From 250,000 to 950,000 the connector commits for 38 to 64% of the wall (64% at 800,000 to 850,000) and spends most of the rest waiting for the next lowest block to be validated: commit groups of 6.8 to 7.5 blocks on average (median 5, at most 32); at 400,000 a group's commit 1.2 ms, the wait before it 1.1 ms, its publish 0.3 ms (47, 42 and 10% of the span); at 820,000 a group's commit 4.9 ms, the wait before it 2.4 ms, its publish 0.5 ms (63, 31 and 6% of the span); at 900,000 2.5, 3.2 and 0.4 ms (41, 52, 7%). About one block in ten (the first of each group) waited on its own validation, 2 to 4.5 ms; the others were validated before their turn and waited for the commit (wait-commit 5 to 12.5 ms a block). A block's residence late in the chain is 54 to 68 ms: read 10 to 14, parse 7 to 8.5, validate 14 to 18, waiting for its commit 10 to 12.5, commit 3.7 to 9.4. With the workers at about 25 core-ms a block at 1,000 blocks/s and the telemetry's 24 to 27 of 32 cores, the run is close to CPU-bound there; the serial commit is the larger share of the connector's time. Above 950,000 (scripts run) a block queues 99 ms for a worker and validates in 128 ms, 216 blocks/s, the commit 7% of the wall: CPU-bound. Below 250,000 the blocks are small (7,000 to 41,000 blocks/s) and the time is fixed per-block costs, a few seconds of the run. Reading: in the heavy stretch the commit is the lever; a wider window would add little with the workers near saturation. By wall time 950,000 to the tip is about 116 s, 800,000 to 950,000 about 155 s, of the 559 s.

Unpruned mainnet sync on the 1 TB volume (25 November 2026, `bin-tip`, the whole datadir on `/data`, `-dbcache=14000`, otherwise as the pruned syncs), stopped at about 837,000 on the maintainer's word: 293,256 at 5 minutes, 401,663 at 10, 462,928 at 15, 517,419 at 20, 571,595 at 25, 617,835 at 30, 657,620 at 35, 695,088 at 40 (behind both pruned runs from 35 minutes on, at the same intake). Sampling per peer showed it download-bound, with the node at 6 of 16 cores: about 170 MB/s from 10 peers, one 15 ms away delivering a third, most 100 to 400 ms away, the two slowest about 2 MB/s each. Banning those two by hand (from 782,560, so the rest of the run is hand-tuned) raised the intake to about 240 MB/s; a replacement 34 ms away delivered 73 MB/s. Banning every peer with a least ping above 120 ms (five, at 812,204) could not be judged: the cache filled at 817,239 and the first write-out run for memory stopped connecting from 14:49:44 to 14:52:00 (71.3 million changes, 7.1 GB, settled at 767,235), the tip moving again only at 14:52:42; the download stood still with it. After it the connect workers waited on coin reads from the volume (27 to 44% I/O wait, 120 to 220 MB/s read), 20 to 40 blocks/s with peers idle behind full request places: the volume (`/dev/sdb`, mounted with `discard`) answered 5,266 reads/s at 4.8 ms each against 0.6 ms on the disk of `/work`, so from 815,000 on the run measured the volume, not the series. Resident set 19 to 20 GB at `-dbcache=14000`. A second run keeps the chainstate on `/work` and only the block files on `/data` (`-blocksdir`): the complete sync below.

Unpruned mainnet sync to 970,000, complete (25 November 2026, 15:05:56 to 17:12:32, `bin-tip`, `-dbcache=14000 -v2transport=0 -onlynet=ipv4`, the chainstate and block index in the job tmp on `/work`, the block files on `/data` through `-blocksdir`; peers tuned throughout, on the maintainer's word that the runs are for the validation architecture's limits, not peer selection: 26 peers banned for a least ping above 120 ms and 24 for delivering under 3 MB/s with blocks in flight from 420,000 on). 2:06:36 (7,596 s) to the stop height; the window validated all 970,002 blocks, no conflict, none retried alone. UTXO set at 970,000 (rolled back offline from 970,263): 165,042,129 txouts, total 20,093,523.00101142, hash_serialized_3 75e7984139ae212468807a917cebed065d02e460f3479b70c1dbc387cef24ce0 (no master run to compare; the total is within some 230 BTC of the subsidy schedule, as the unspendable and unclaimed outputs make it). At the end 822 GB of block and undo files and a 13 GB chainstate; resident set up to about 20.7 GB.

| Elapsed | Height | What set the pace |
|---|---|---|
| 0 to 5 min | 0 to 258,112 | the per-block work (small blocks, few requests outstanding) |
| 5 to 50 min | 258,112 to 800,983 | the download: about 180 blocks/s; peers delivered 180 to 240 MB/s with the request places full; from about 640,000 the volume's write bandwidth: 221 MB/s written at 82 ms a write, 89% busy, the download window full (requests at tip+902 to tip+1006), the connect workers at 20% each |
| 50 to 66 min | 800,983 to 870,177 | the cache filled at about 817,000; the first write-out for memory (not logged, before `coindb` was turned on), then coin reads from `/work` |
| 66 to 85 min | 870,177 to 886,427 | a second write-out, 16:09:37 to 16:11:33 (70.9 million changes, 7.1 GB, 1:56 against 2:16 with the chainstate on the volume), then 8 to 12 blocks/s: the connect workers in disk wait on coin reads, `/work` 99% busy at 17,500 to 20,500 reads/s and 780 to 920 MB/s, about 48 KB read for each lookup (read-ahead on LevelDB's mapped tables, inferred) |
| 85 to 115 min | 886,427 to 955,669 | the cache warming again: 25 rising to 62 blocks/s, `/work` busy 98% falling to 88% |
| 115 to 127 min | 955,669 to 970,000 | the final periodic write, 17:04:56 to 17:08:28 (121.5 million changes, 12.2 GB, final), connecting stopped |

So on this host the series' sync is bound in turn by the per-block work, the peers, the volume's write bandwidth and, once a cache below the UTXO set fills, by coin reads on the chainstate's disk, whose read amplification after each write-out (half the cache evicted) dominates the last fifth of the chain; the connect pool had cores to spare throughout. The maintainer's `-reindex-chainstate` at the same cache (12:49.29) shows what the same lookups cost on a local NVMe.

### 7.2 The flush: probes and the estimate

All probes throwaway, 12 million coins, on this VM; directions only.

- Order of the batches (32 MiB batches, on-disk database with the 8 MiB cache): hash order 28 s with 3.2 GB of compaction writes; disjoint descending batches unsorted inside 27.4 s with 0.86 GB; disjoint and sorted 5.7 s; the store's flush as built then 10.2 s. The time on the VM was the database's insertion of each key into its memtable, which a sorted batch makes about five times cheaper.
- Whole shards and four workers (a shard about 1.3 batches): 9.84 s before, 8.45 s without the cut inside a shard, 7.54 s with four workers as well. With four workers the flush is bound by the database's single writer.

The estimate for the maintainer's machine was about 40 s against 145 s; the 16:36.45 run measured 42.84 s.

### 7.3 CPU per transaction, the profile, the trials

The reference, chain C, 16 cores, all-thread CPU per transaction:

| run | blocks/s | threads on CPU | CPU per tx |
|---|---|---|---|
| pool off (`-prevoutfetchthreads=0`) | 25.1 | 1.49 | 19.8 µs (connector alone 12.9) |
| below the window (`-connectwindow=1`), 16 workers | 26.4 | 2.18 | 27.5 µs |
| window, 1 worker | 31.6 | 1.03 | 10.9 µs |
| window, 2 workers | 56.2 | 2.03 | 12.0 µs |
| window, 8 workers | 229.6 | 7.95 | 11.6 µs |
| window, 16 workers | 416.7 | 14.38 | 11.7 µs |

The window path's CPU per transaction is flat from one worker to sixteen and no higher than either serial path. On the maintainer's machine it was 13.5 to 22 µs then (per phase late: read 3.2, fetch 1.9, core 4.0, batch 4.0, cont 3.3, the block's free 2.4), of the size two threads per core and mainnet's larger set would give (inference).

Profile of the tip of 11 November (chain D, 16 workers, workers only). CPU: map lookups 33.3%, allocation and free 21.8%, SHA-256 5.9%, lock and yield 3.4%. Wall: 9.0% of samples in a system call, from the allocator's lock on cross-thread frees of nodes (`EraseDetached` under `SpendLocked` under `ApplyBatch`) and the shard lock taken by `PeekCoinFor` from core and cont. The duplicate `HaveInputs` in core is 0.54%.

The CPU probe on the maintainer's machine (14 November, beside the 14:18.66 run of the huge page trial). Counters over 5 s, all threads: instructions per cycle 0.49 at 600,000, 0.48 at 820,000, 0.41 at 900,000 and 2.26 at 970,000 (scripts); second-level data TLB misses 2.5, 2.2 and 3.0 million per CPU-second below assumevalid. The connect is bound by memory stalls, the script stretch by arithmetic (secp256k1 about 85% of worker samples there).

Huge pages: the process had 5.8 of 10.7 GiB anonymous memory in huge pages at 600,000, 10.5 of 22.7 at 820,000, 11.0 of 28.7 at 900,000 (54%, 46%, 38%); between 820,000 and 900,000 the store grew 6.0 GiB and 0.55 GiB of it was huge (269 huge faults granted, 10,026 fallen back). `enabled` and `defrag` are both `madvise`; free memory was 5.0, 7.0 and 1.1 GiB with 34, 20 and 20 GiB of page cache (the block files, read once), and the kernel reclaimed and compacted in the faulting threads (direct reclaim 23.6 million pages and 88,500 compaction stalls between 600,000 and 900,000). So the page cache of block files takes the memory the huge pages would need. On the harness host (aarch64, glibc 2.43, huge pages in "madvise" mode), on the empty chain with glibc's own hint off: master 0 kB of `AnonHugePages`, the huge page commit 6144 kB, the nodes of 40,000 coins. glibc 2.43 hints all of malloc's memory by default on that box (master: 137 MiB huge of 166 MiB resident on the empty chain, 0 of 74 MiB with `GLIBC_TUNABLES=glibc.malloc.hugetlb=0`), so harness figures from it were taken with huge pages already; that default is glibc 2.43's for AArch64 only (its NEWS; Ubuntu 26.04's package changelog carries no change to it), so on x86-64 malloc memory is unhinted unless the tunable `glibc.malloc.hugetlb=1` (glibc 2.35 or later) is set.

Worker samples at 900,000 by function (four workers, 3 s; 600,000 and 820,000 are alike): glibc malloc and free 26.1% (`_int_malloc` 6.4, `_int_free_merge_chunk` 5.8, `unlink_chunk` 4.9, `__libc_malloc2` 4.2, `cfree` 3.0, the rest 1.8), with the destructors that call them 6.7% more (`~vector<CTxIn>` 3.4, `CTransaction` 1.9, the bin's `Empty` 1.3); the callers the chains show are first the witness stacks (a vector per stack and one per item, allocated in `ReadBlock`, freed in the bin) and the per-block set of txids that `ConnectBlock` builds at the first deferred transaction (a node per transaction, at least 1.2%). The block view's map 17 to 18% (`try_emplace` 5.3 + 2.2 + 1.2, its pool's nodes 2.6, `ApplyChanges` walking it 4.7, part of the hasher's 2.8). The store 18% (`Detach` 4.6, `Map::find` 4.7, `ApplyBatch` 3.0, `FetchFor` 1.7, node allocation 1.1, try-lock 1.1, `try_emplace` 0.8); of `Map::find`, 0.85 points are single probes from `HaveInputs` called by `ConnectBlock` in the first pass, for coins the fetch had already not found; 1.04 are the continuation's by their chain, and the 2.12 whose chain ends at `HaveInputs < CheckTxInputs` are very likely the continuation's too, since in the first pass with deferral on `CheckTxInputs` finds every coin in the view (`ConnectBlock`'s own `HaveInputs` fetched it). So about 3.2 points are the continuation's single lookups, which the batched fetch for a continuation addresses (7.4), not the first pass. (An earlier reading here gave the first pass 3.0 points; it was wrong.) Size computation of transactions 2.9% (`SerializeTransaction<SizeComputer>`), SHA-256 2.3%, `GetSigOpCount` 1.5%. The call chains were cut at 1400 characters, so many allocations have no caller shown; the flat profile of all threads was lost below assumevalid to a sort by thread (fixed in the script).

Reading: the allocator's share at mainnet's scale is larger than the harness profile's 21.8% and it is not lock waits any more (7.4) but CPU in glibc's slow paths (chunks merged and unlinked, so not served by the per-thread cache). Three shares of about a fifth each (allocator, view map, store) and a huge page coverage of 38% late are what a run below 10 minutes has to come from; the script stretch (about 100 s) and the flush (36 s) have no lever.

The three trials of 14 November (chain D regenerated, 16 workers, `-dbcache=1024`, BIP30 switch, five alternating rounds, `cpu/ab2.py`; worker CPU in ms a heavy block from the log's phase lines):

| state | blocks/s, median | worker CPU | core | cont | deferred | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| tip 26bd9d762b | 510 | 26.9 to 27.2 | 11.6 to 11.7 | 2.1 to 2.3 | 107,000 to 113,000 | |
| + txid vector | 1.02x and 1.06x | 0.96x in both sessions | 11.0 to 11.1 | 2.11 | the same | on the series then |
| + no second tier probe for an input the fetch found no entry for | 0.94x of the state above | 1.03x of the state above | 11.18 | 3.07 | 188,000 (1.76x) | dropped (86616d2b1a) |
| + input coins by position, without the skip | 1.086x of the tip, 1.07x of the txid vector | 0.91x of the tip, 0.95x of the txid vector | 9.83 (0.89x of the txid vector) | 2.02 | the same | on the series then; dropped without a mainnet gain (13:59.75, 7.1; 8.1) |

Why the skip loses: the fetch runs at the start of a block's task and the single probe some milliseconds later, and in between lower blocks apply their creations. The second probe of the tier was finding about 80,000 of those coins a run; with the tier's answer remembered they are missed, the transaction is deferred and the continuation looks every one of its inputs up singly. The probe saved was 0.85 points at most. The skip was built so that only outpoints with no entry at all are remembered (a spent marker is read again) and only where deferral is on; it is correct and not worth having. The batched fetch for a continuation (1e17b4a574, 14 November; the maintainer: "Lets try out the batched continuation fetch"): `BlockApplyView::Prefetch(block, txs)` called by `RunContinuation()` before `FinishDeferredTransactions()`. Chain D against the 47-commit tip, same settings, five alternating rounds: cont CPU 2.00 to 1.82 ms a heavy block (0.91x), worker CPU 24.76 to 24.29 (0.981x), rate 1.012x (noise), deferred the same. Mainnet defers 27.2% against the chain's 12.6% and the continuation was 19% of worker CPU there, so the share addressed is larger, but after the span commits no harness ratio is carried over: the mainnet run (14:01.22, 7.1) showed no gain.

The harness caveat of 6.3 holds for the two kept: a small store, so the ratios are upper bounds for mainnet. The maintainer's run with them (13:59.75, 7.1) showed user CPU 0.986x and no wall gain: the probes the span removes are repeats of a probe whose first hit took the memory stall, cheap where the first one is not, which the harness with its small store does not weigh (an inference from the 0.41 to 0.49 instructions per cycle, not measured).

Trials (on a throwaway branch):

| trial | chain D, 16 workers | 2 workers, cache not binding | 2 workers, cache binding | memory | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| T1 pool per shard (upstream's `PoolResource`) | 1.21 to 1.26x | 1.19 to 1.21x | 1.07x | +10 to +40 MiB, pools 1.55 times the live nodes | dropped for its memory |
| T1p a thread keeps the nodes it frees, bound 1024 | 1.12x | 1.19x | 0.98x (noise) | as the tip | on the series (4.3) |
| T1p, bound 8192 | about 1.2x | 1.20x | 1.01x (noise) | +10 to +20 MiB | not taken |
| T3 plain shard mutex (retry lock removed) | 1.015x and 0.99x (noise) | 1.01x (noise) | | equal | held then: no measured loss on 16 cores, but a waiter sleeps more often (13 to 31 switches a block), which could show at more threads. Taken on 22 November after the maintainer's run at 32 workers, 10:33.33 (5.2, 7.1) |
| T4 duplicate `HaveInputs` | not built | | | | dropped, 0.54% |
| T2 batched fetch for a continuation | not built then; see 7.4 | | | | at the noise limit |

Block bins and own hash on chain C, reindex: 1.13x at sixteen workers, 1.08x at eight, with less CPU; neither alone does it (the bin alone 1.05x, own hash alone 0.90x at eight workers). Sync at four cores 1.04x. Pool on a CPU 79% to 88%, voluntary switches a block 85 to 21. With the two the best pool width is the core count and more is worse; before them more workers than cores gained a little because workers slept on hash futures.

#### The block file read-ahead (14 November; dropped, 8.1)

The maintainer, on the dip between 600,000 and 800,000 (7.1): "Just try out the block reads and the serial publisher", memory growth being accepted as a cost. No code gave the operating system a read-ahead hint, and each block is read by its worker with an open, an 8 byte read and the block's read. The connector thread asked for the block files up to four ahead of the one a staged block is in (`BlockManager::ReadAheadBlockFile()`, `posix_fadvise(WILLNEED)`), as a front task on the connect pool. Linux (5.15 on the dev box, read_ahead_kb 128) cuts one request down to its read-ahead window: one call for a 127 MiB file cached 320 pages, the same file in 128 KiB steps was cached whole within 0.29 s for 43 ms of calls, so the function steps.

Harness (`ab3.py`, `EVICT=1` drops chain D's block files from the page cache before each run, 16 workers, four rounds): read wall 6.82 ms a block against 9.07 (cached: 6.73), 526.6 against 496.9 blocks/s (1.06x, ranges apart); cached, three rounds, 521.0 against 511.8, inside the spread. The harness shows that the mechanism works and costs nothing warm; its chain is three block files on a fast disk, so it says nothing about the size of the effect on the maintainer's block store, where what share of the blocks was in the page cache during the runs is not known.

The maintainer then asked whether it works at all, blocks being stored in arrival order, not by height (the harness chain is stored by height, so this was not exercised). The files are asked for in file order, each once, up to four above the highest file a staged block was in; as first committed, a block whose file lay more than four above the mark made the files in between be skipped, which a late arrival (up to the download window of 1024 blocks, about a dozen files) does. A fix: the request started at the mark, at most sixteen files below the block (`READ_AHEAD_BEHIND_FILES`). What remained by design: the file of a late arrival was asked for only when that block was staged, so that one block was read cold. The maintainer's run with it, 13:49.90, was "not really faster" (7.1); it is not on the series. The publisher's two trials (pool size, worker priority) are in 5.2 and showed nothing on the harness.

### 7.4 Where the workers' idle time went

What holds by construction: a store entry is made by one thread and released by another, since the block that creates an output and the block that spends it run on different workers. Allocator literature names remote frees as the hard case (10). glibc's per-thread cache takes seven objects per size (`glibc.malloc.tcache_count`) and serves a free from another thread under the allocating thread's arena lock. The idle time was a sum of microsecond gaps whose cost follows the number of sleeps, not their length (a park costs 7 to 10 microseconds).

Thread counters per worker on the maintainer's machine (32 workers, before the depot) showed two regimes: a baseline of about 10% blocked in parks of about 8 µs where the set does not grow, and 7 to 12 points more in parks of about 27 µs where it grows. The continuation was about half of all off-CPU time (4.6 of 9.0 ms a block at 550k to 800k, 7.6 of 16.1 ms at 800k to 950k).

The idle probe (thread states, system calls and park stacks of four workers), per connect worker before and after the depot:

| | early before | early after | late before | late after |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| on a CPU | 79.9% | 86.8% | 77.0% | 89.3% |
| waiting for a core | 7.4% | 6.7% | 5.4% | 8.8% |
| asleep | 12.8% | 6.5% | 17.5% | 1.9% |
| parks a second | 9,900 | 2,470 | 11,900 | 3,080 |
| futex, share of wall | 17 to 20% | 6 to 7.5% | 20 to 26% | 3.7 to 4.8% |

Before: 83 to 88% of all parks were the glibc arena lock in free or malloc: the free of a map cell beyond the kept bound in `Map::erase` under the shard mutex (58 to 63%, most from `RunContinuation`), the old `CTxUndo` coin vectors in `SettleUndo` (10 to 16%), the destruction of a `CBlock` in the bins (7 to 9%). No wait for work above 0.3%. The workers did not idle for lack of work, they queued for the allocator.

After, parks left late: map cell free past the depot 63% (about 1,950 a second, before 6,900), undo vectors in `SettleUndo` 10%, witness byte vectors 5%, block destruction 3%, shard mutex 2%, block file read 2%. The cell free still reaches glibc two thousand times a second late, so the depot is at its 64 lists much of the time there; why is not counted. All futex time is under 5% of a worker's wall, which bounds what any further allocator change gives late; the larger non-CPU share is now waiting for a core.

Further trials, none on the series:

- The batched fetch for a continuation (18 lines): cont wall 0.91x, cont CPU 0.92x, block rate equal on the harness.
- The read phase. `BlockManager::ReadBlock` on the worker: open the file, a zeroed buffer, the read with the xor, `DeserializeBlock` (txid and wtxid hashed from the bytes by the worker, `make_shared` `CTransaction`), header hash and proof of work; 28% of late worker CPU then (14.1 ms a block). Harness wall profile of the phase: SHA256 38.7%, allocation while deserialising 30.2%, parsing 17.9%, copy from the file 5.3%. A sizing probe with wrong wtxids: read CPU 0.81x, all phases' CPU 0.965x. The wtxid is the one part the connect does not need for a block read from disk below assumevalid; it cannot be skipped, only computed on first use, a change to `CTransaction` (a const member today, read without synchronisation everywhere). The allocation share is the layout of `CTransaction` and has no small fix.
- Input coins looked up once and passed by position (two commits, 157 lines). `CCoinsViewCache::AccessInputs(tx, coins)` looks each input's coin up once and returns pointers in input order; `Consensus::CheckTxInputs`, `GetP2SHSigOpCount`, `GetTransactionSigOpCost` and `CheckInputScripts` get an overload taking those coins and the view-taking forms call it. Probes per input fall from six or seven to two. Harness then: core CPU 0.915x at 16 workers and 0.90x at two, the worker as a whole 0.965 to 0.97x, rate within noise; parked. Taken up again on 14 November after the mainnet probe showed the view's map at 17 to 18% of worker samples, on the series that day, and dropped without a mainnet gain (13:59.75, 7.1; 8.1).
- Moving the confirm frees to a worker (talked, nothing built). No gain on the maintainer's machine (the workers bind; 130 s of publisher CPU would move, not vanish); on more cores it raises the point where the serial thread binds. The maintainer's objection holds: a free on a thread other than the allocating one queues for that arena's lock, the record is already a cross-thread free, and only a free by the worker that built the record avoids it, while a continuation may run elsewhere. Revisit when a machine shows the publisher as the bound.

The fall from 8 to 16 workers on chain E is not the window width (no trend from 8 to 64), not the BIP30 lookups (they cost at four workers, nothing at eight) and not the markers (off: every rate rises, the fall stays between 12 and 16). It is more runnable threads than cores: a chain E block is so small that the publisher (50 to 90 µs a block), the scheduler thread with the validation callbacks (about 23 µs, three sleeps a block) and the effects executor (about 12 µs) are each about as busy as a worker and have no core of their own. Confined to 8 cores the peak is at 6 workers (12060 to 13200 against 8970 to 9640 at 8); on 16 cores 13430 to 15160 at 12 against 10450 to 12090 at 16. With work in the blocks it does not carry over: chain D 397-409 / 442-464 / 444-475 / 431-496 at 12 / 14 / 15 / 16 workers. The default of one thread a core is right where the time goes.

Open and unsampled: one block with far more script work than its neighbours holds the window while the other workers run out of blocks; master spreads one block's scripts over all threads and has no such case.

### 7.5 The receive side measured

**The local harness** (one or more local peers; a local peer is never the bound, and the paused sender cannot be reproduced with its small blocks).

- Before the receive pool, on a sync from one local peer (twelve client cores), the syncing node's message handler was 0.87 of a core on CPU and `Deserialize block` was 3.5 ms of the 5.5 ms block period; the serving node's handler was at 0.26.
- With it: 2.15 to 2.2x at twelve cores (390 against 180 blocks/s), 1.15x at four, 1.0x at two; a reindex unaffected. The handler is at 0.04 of a core and the pool is the bound; at four cores the pool is CPU-bound.
- The next request at the hand-off: hand-off to next request, median 137 to 29 microseconds at 12 cores (p99 7595 to 569), 293 to 18 at 2 cores (p99 12740 to 688); blocks on request from the peer at a hand-off 11.5 to 12.0 before, 14.95 after. No request twice. Throughput on the harness unchanged. No new test.
- One, two and four peers, each serving node on two cores of its own, give the same rate (433 to 457 blocks/s at 8 workers): blocks arriving out of order from several peers cost nothing on our side.

**Benchcoin** (PR 201 of bitcoin-dev-tools/benchcoin; 8 cores and 16 threads). It copies a pruned data directory at height 840,000, drops the page cache and syncs to 900,000 from one remote peer (`-connect`) with `-prune=1000000`, at `-dbcache=450` and `32000`, each uninstrumented and under perf; the range is under assumevalid. The log held a sequence of readings, most withdrawn by the next (8.2); what survived:

- Walls. The fast level of the branch is 1643 to 1698 s over several code states, and its fastest profile is 1643 s on three of them; master's nightly is 2010 to 2100 s, with levels of its own that flip between nights on unchanged code. A difference between two runs of the branch inside its band says nothing about the commits. Run 43120525279 (with the receive pool and the request at the hand-off): 1643.5 / 1675.1 / 1656.8 / 1775.2 s, at the fast level of the runs before the receive pool and not below it; process CPU 3.5 of 16 threads, 27.4 ms a block. Master is at about 0.8 of this level, so this benchmark can show at most about 1.25x over master for any change behind the socket.
- The slow profiles of the run without the receive pool (42992592996) are an outlier by the maintainer's verdict (5.6).
- The node waits for blocks; the connect is not the bound (artifact of run 42992592996). A block is connected within 2 s of its arrival; no thread is near full; I/O pressure is 1.4% of the wall. When the publisher waits for a write-out run (85 s and 175 s a profile), blocks go on arriving at the same rate, the backlog grows to 30 to 50 blocks, and afterwards the connect publishes 70 to 95 blocks in a second against a mean of 32: the waits are absorbed by slack and cost no wall.
- Throughput follows bytes, not blocks: about 61 MB/s at the fast level whatever the block size. The link is not the limit: a block's bytes flow at 150 MB/s or more while they arrive and the wire is idle for most of each cycle. What limits is a wait in each block's cycle of request and delivery of about 19 to 22 ms that does not depend on the block's size. What sets it was not established; nothing in the data sees the serving side, and the round trip to the peer was never measured.
- In the 450 profiles the socket thread is silent 0.40 to 0.65 s about every 20 s while a connect thread runs `EvictClean` under `FlushStateToDisk`, about 40 s a profile.
- The serving loop as master's code has it (that the peer runs Core is an assumption): a serving node sets `fPauseSend` when its send buffer exceeds 1,000,000 bytes, its handler then skips that peer, the socket thread clears the flag without waking the handler, and the handler sleeps up to 100 ms unless a complete message arrives. So the serving node goes on at our next getdata or after up to 100 ms. In a third of the stalls of the artifact the stream halted about 100 ms after our requests stopped.
- The receive pool as first built cost 1.5 to 1.6 times the CPU a block of handler plus hash threads; not re-measured after the two CPU commits.
- Lessons kept as rules: a thread's share on CPU is no saturation test for a thread that blocks on I/O, and a wide on-CPU frame is no bottleneck; do not swing to the opposite reading untested either.

**The network simulation** (`/work/utxo-partial-order-harness/net`: `netsim.sh`, `relay.py`, profiles). Regtest, 1600 heavy blocks (3000 transactions of two inputs and two outputs, about 1.1 MB on the wire), empty blocks on top for assumevalid. Chain M (`build_chain_m.py`) uses spend ages the maintainer supplied (about 52% of spends under an hour old, about 23% of the same block, about 79% under a day): 23% of inputs spend an output of the same block, 29% one 1 to 6 blocks old, 27% one 7 to 144 blocks old, 21% older, log-uniform inside a band (an assumption). Eight serving nodes on the unmodified master binary, one core each, each behind a user-space relay with a one-way delay and a rate cap towards the client; they stay near idle. The client on 8 cores, v2 transport. The measure is heavy blocks a second from the tip updates. Not modelled: real TCP (slow start, loss), real peers' disks, outbound peer selection; the peers are manual connections, which are paused, not disconnected, when they stall.

Profiles, fixed before the branch was measured: delays 5 to 100 ms; rates in MB/s 3M (12, 10, 10, 8, 8, 6, 4, 2), 6M (twice that), 6Ms (as 6M with the last peer at 0.5), mix (30, 20, 10, 5, 2, 1, 0.5, 0.25), delay (no cap), open (no delay, no cap).

Master is bound by its one message thread (44.0 of 45.0 s on CPU) at about 20 blocks a second on any profile that carries that much. The series before the request rule is link-bound with eight healthy peers and loses most with slow ones: every peer is asked for sixteen blocks whatever its speed, a slow peer's sixteen sit at the lower end of the 1024-block download window, the fast peers fill the window and then idle while the tip moves at the slow peer's pace.

Blocks a second, chain M, one run each:

| profile | link capacity | master | before the rule | with the rule | rule / master |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 3M | 55 | 20.5 | 44.4 | 53.4 | 2.6x |
| 6M | 109 | 20.5 | 88.9 | 101.3 | 4.9x |
| 6Ms | 110 | 16.7 | 77.3 | 100.5 | 6.0x |
| mix | 62 | 13.8 | 31.8 | 78.4 | 5.7x |
| open | none | 20.7 | 191.1 | 184.6 | 8.9x |
| delay | none | 20.9 | 185.5 | 188.4 | 9.0x |

On the capped profiles the rule is at 90 to 97% of what the links carry. The mix profile is two-valued between single runs (64.6 and 78.4 with the rule, 39.9 and 31.8 before) and gives no fine verdict. With the client on two cores the client's CPU is the bound and the ratio to master was 1.8 (chain N, BIP30 check on, not redone). With a single peer at 100 ms each way and no cap the rate is sixteen blocks per round trip (86.7 blocks a second); asking for more than sixteen is treated as outside the receive-only rule and was not tried. Not tested: other values of the two seconds, a real network.

**A friend's mainnet sync** of the series (default log sampled read-only): 133 minutes to height 975,073, 32 s shutdown with a 25.5 GiB cache of 168 million coins. To height 463,921 about 23 minutes (about 335 blocks/s), from there 110 minutes (77 blocks/s). Long-run rate 115 to 124 blocks/s on 1 MB blocks (about 1 Gbit/s), later 90 on larger blocks; per second it swings between 0 and 400: pauses of one to three seconds, each followed by a burst that repays it. Catch-up at up to 413 blocks/s, so the connect has over three times the headroom asked of it. Closing lines: 975,163 blocks of peers on the connect pool, 747,142 validated in the window (64 wide, 16 connect threads), 310 million transactions deferred, 0 conflicts, block cache 416,014 of 975,088. No master run beside it, so no speedup figure; against a line floor of about 100 minutes at 1 Gbit/s (chain size from memory) the run is about a third above it.

Reading of the pauses: every peer holds at least one block (the request rule limits the count, not which blocks), blocks are handed out lowest first whatever the peer's speed, so a slow peer regularly holds the lowest missing block and the tip stands until it arrives. From the first "Peer is stalling block download" line on the pauses cost time, since master's stall rule fires only once the 1024-block window is full.

**The front-stall trial** (2027ea7fb8, 15 lines in `net_processing.cpp`; not on the series). In initial block download a peer's stall starts as soon as a block it has yet to send is the lowest one missing and at least 32 downloaded blocks wait behind a missing one (`m_blocks_unlinked`). The existing machinery does the rest unchanged: the timer is cleared by the peer's next block, after the adaptive timeout (2 s, doubling per stall) the peer is disconnected, or paused for two minutes if manual. The 32 make it relative: on a line where all peers are slow nothing fires. Harness (chain M; all harness peers are `-connect` peers, so a stall pauses the peer and no replacement exists):

| profile | variant | blocks/s | longest pause | time in pauses over 0.5 s |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| crawl (six fast peers, one at 0.3, one at 0.05 MB/s) | rule off | 78.7, 79.1 | 12.0 s | 13.0 s |
| crawl | 2 s timeout | 99.6, 99.8 | 2.7 to 3.2 s | 5.4 to 6.0 s |
| crawl | 0.5 s | 99.7 | 1.7 s | 1.7 s |
| slow3 (six fast, then 2, 1, 0.5 MB/s) | rule off | 104.0 | 1.2 s | 6.2 s |
| slow3 | 2 s | 104.6 | 1.1 s | 6.0 s |
| slow3 | 0.5 s | 107.9 | 0.9 s | 1.4 s |

Where a slow peer fills the window the rule gives 1.27x at the existing 2 s; where it does not the rate is unchanged and only a shorter timeout smooths the tip (a timeout under a second needs the timeout's type changed from whole seconds). Open, for the maintainer: `test/functional/p2p_ibd_stalling.py` fails on the trial at its first step ("a staller does not get disconnected if the 1024 block lookahead buffer is filled"), since it asserts master's rule; by C16 that is a finding against the design. The replacement of a disconnected peer can be measured only on mainnet. The alternative of a second request is in 5.2 (the stalled-front row) and 5.4 (S1).

### 7.6 The design review of 2026-11-13

Asked: go over the whole design again, interrogate the memory and parallelism tradeoffs and pruning, and say whether a different design reaches equal or better performance with every constraint intact. Seven passes: constraints (section 2), inventory (4.1), memory, parallelism, pruning, alternatives, synthesis (5.1, 5.2). On the maintainer's word the passes stayed on the design level: no measurement, no code. Only what the verdicts of 5.1 and 5.2 rest on is kept.

**Memory.** Outside `-dbcache`, in MiB at 2 / 16 threads, late mainnet blocks taken at 5.4 MB in memory (I): the window 22 / 56 at a 450 MiB cache, 128 at 1 GiB, 173 with a large cache (4.5); the block cache 128 whatever the cache; receive tasks 6 / 90; block bins 22 / up to 173, counted by no budget and the weakest figure; kept-node lists 0.5 / 4; the depot 8; the hash pool 3 / 12 (since removed); the undo of published blocks until the effects thread wrote it, at most 64 MiB serialised. Sum about 190 MiB at 2 threads, about 470 MiB at 16 with a 450 MiB cache and 540 MiB at 1 GiB. A flush worker adds its shard (moved, not copied) and one batch.

- At the small default the memory outside the budget is of the size of the budget (I); the harness never showed it because its blocks are small. This is the largest open point against C4 and a matter of accounting, not mechanism. The thread default multiplies receive tasks, bins and kept lists by the core count without regard to memory. The low-end case, four cores with 4 GB, sums to about 220 MiB (I); never measured.
- The one byte budget of 5.1, derived from `-dbcache` with `-blockcache` as its override, would replace four bounds and take the thread count out of the memory question; its price is the block cache's gain on the network path (row 6 of 4.1) where the budget is small. Dropped since (8.1).
- Per coin 147 B on a real run against 138 B for master, about 6% fewer coins for the same bytes (I). The cell was then 128 bytes and 144 from glibc; the compact cell since is 88 bytes, about 95 bytes a coin on mainnet (4.2, 5.8, 7.1).
- Resident memory on the maintainer's machine is the highest cache size plus about 2 GiB (freed cells stay with the allocator, about 0.5 GiB is the window). The maintainer: "totally fine then".
- The batch (peak resident memory on chain M on the network harness, branch and master, at 450 MiB, 1 GiB and 8 GiB with 2 and 16 threads) ran: 7.7.

**Parallelism.** Beside the connect pool at one thread a core, the helpers (hash pool, effects, connector, handler, socket, the database's background thread) are nearly idle by measurement, except the socket thread at a high link rate and the database's background thread at the prune ceiling. So the pool is oversubscribed by one to two busy threads; that costs rate only on blocks of a few dozen transactions (7.4), and the default stays. Of the four factors for the late connect, the share on CPU is at 89 to 92% (at most about 1.1x left), CPU per transaction is in 7.3, and the serial parts are the flush, the publisher's own time (7.1) and the blocks above assumevalid. On a real sync the connect is not the bound (7.5); on two to four cores CPU per transaction counts, not width.

- The connector thread stays (it lets the handler and the receive tasks hand blocks on without activating); the flush helpers stay at up to four; the window cap of 64 stays (only machines above 32 cores see it). The hash pool and the effects thread were measured since (7.7); the pool size and the retry lock are in 5.2.
- Deferral: 310 million transactions deferred and no conflict on mainnet, about a quarter of all (I), each costing one database read that finds nothing; deferral has since left the series with the read-only window (4.5).
- The low end with a binding cache (6.3) was not profiled then; the harness profile since is in 7.7 ("The low-end corner profiled").

**Pruning.** No pruned sync of the series was measured in a way that isolates the node.

- Two mechanisms keep the written head above what pruning may delete: the lag ceiling (4.10), and the prune flush, which before a file is unlinked writes out past the last prunable block. The second is sufficient where it holds; no crash sequence could be constructed in which the ceiling saves a node the prune flush does not. Not closed: that every path that unlinks files goes through that flush (the two automatic finders, the manual prune RPC, the pin), and the replay length after a crash.
- With the ceiling a pruned node writes every coin that lives longer than about 129 to 256 blocks. Without it a prune flush writes only up to the prune height, at least 288 blocks below the tip, and with 79% of spends under a day old most coins of the stretch never reach the database, where master writes every dirty coin up to the tip at every prune event (it keeps its cache: `Sync()` at a prune flush on e211dd0186; this passage said it emptied the cache, corrected 2026-11-18). On paper the series without the ceiling writes less than master on a pruned sync and with it more (I): the largest expected low-end gain of the review, and a removal.
- `EvictClean` in one piece silences the socket thread (7.5); a bounded amount per block on the confirming worker would spread it.
- The batch (a pruned sync against master on the network harness with files deleted) ran, and the reading of the prune paths found two gaps the ceiling hid; the ceiling is gone: 4.10, 7.7.

**Alternatives.** Inside the design, every component is "keep" except those in 5.1 and 5.2. Two comparisons carry the answer:

- Master's cache with inputs fetched in parallel ahead of a serial apply is worse on a reindex by measurement (7.3). On a real sync it is not ruled out, since there the delivery is the bound; master's connect rate on real hardware, unknown then, is 2:10:38 (7.1).
- The independent design. A fresh agent designed from the goal, the constraints and seven facts, without the series (its memory index carried one-line summaries of the branch, so agreement is weak evidence). It agrees on the receive side, the key-range shards with sorted batches and memory inside `-dbcache`. It differs at the centre: no deferral. Its window is a batch of consecutive blocks; each block emits its creations and spends per shard in block order, one task per shard applies them in order, and the checks run per block in parallel on the coins found. That has no missing input, no continuation, no record of an open block and no retry rule, at the price of a barrier at each window, every surviving coin inserted twice and cs_main held through the ordered phase. It also writes the database only between windows by master's flush, which drops the low-end write-out logic the maintainer said to keep. Verdict: no cheaper way to the same place for the low end; deferral is one of two ways to order the inputs and the other is simpler, but changing over would rewrite the series' centre, and the BIP30 gap it would have closed by construction is closed (4.6).

The external designs (sources in 10; figures self-reported), each with the constraint it breaks: Utreexo and UHS need a protocol change, against receive-only; SwiftSync needs a hints file, a new input; Libbitcoin gives up pruning and the small machine; Hornet (48 B a coin against 147 B here then, in a compact index over an append-only file) gives up `-dbcache` and the small machine; Core issue 36476 adds trust in the assumevalid range. None reaches the goal inside the constraints.

Ruben Somsen, "Out-of-Order Validation & The Single Spend Check" (gist RubenSomsen/21c45b1c57448881ce5007d884fb3f21, 2026, a summary of the 2140 Bitcoin Consensus Code Week, given by the maintainer on 19 November), read against the series:

- Its logical design (headers first, blocks downloaded in order within a moving window, integrity checked on arrival, transactions validated in any order once a gap-free run of blocks back to genesis is in) is the series' shape already: the request window, the off-lock check and store of a received block, and the connect window validating blocks beside each other once they follow the tip without a gap. Its argument for not going further (validation is bound by bandwidth or by hardware, and waiting for the blocks below costs nothing either way) is the series' own reason for a window of a fixed width rather than validation in arbitrary order.
- Double spends: it optimises for their absence and orders only when one occurs, the series' choice too: blocks apply out of order, the store tells two spends of a coin apart by height (the lower stands, the higher block gets a conflict), and a conflict sends the block back to be connected alone, which is its "another pass to find the double spend if it occurred".
- The single spend check (a spent bit per output, set without reading, spends counted against inputs; or unspent bits against outputs minus inputs) is wait-free but needs a set that keeps spent outputs, and the document itself grants that pruning spent outputs, which the series must do for the small machine (C4), loses most of its benefit, and that exclusive access by compare-and-swap or sharded locks is fine in practice. The series' store is the conventional kind (256 shards, a lock each); the workers' parks were measured as mostly the allocator's lock, not the shards' (7.4: 83 to 88% the glibc arena before the depot), so a wait-free set would buy little here. What carries over is the counting as a cheap global check, which is P10 (5.4, a salted aggregate of creations minus spends as a test oracle; not built).
- Adding each block's outputs to a set on arrival, before validation, is the window matching trial of 19 November (8.1): built, correct, and not faster on the harness, because deferral was already cheap.
- Its output-caching figures (about a third of outputs spent in their own block, and of the rest 9 / 31 / 66 / 83% within 1 / 6 / 144 / 2016 blocks, from mainnet.observer) are of the same shape as the spend ages recorded here (7.5: 23% in the same block, 52% within an hour, 79% within a day; counted differently). They support the lag keeping young coins in memory; nothing new to build.
- Separating signature checks from the rest of the script check, for batch or GPU verification, is the one direction the series has not touched. It matters only above assumevalid, where scripts run (and at the tip, where the signature cache already holds most of them, C3); the series' measurements are below it. A possible later step, outside the current goal; not proposed.
- SwiftSync, compared again: its hints file is a new input, against C2 ("no hints") and the receive-only rule, as recorded above; the document's case that the hints' source is trusted only against wasted time, not for validity, does not change that constraint.

Conclusion: the document describes the design the series already is, by a different road; its novel part, the single spend check, does not fit a store that prunes, and its counting survives only as the P10 oracle. Nothing in it calls for a change to the series.

### 7.7 The harness against master: the baseline, the trials, the grids

All H: chain M, the client on 8 cores unless stated, the BIP30 switch, alternating runs; heavy-section blocks/s. The scripts are in the harness directory; binaries and datadirs are gone.

**Memory and the rival** (network sync, eight master peers, open profile). Master is master at its best: `-par=8 -prevoutfetchthreads=16` (its inputs are fetched in parallel since `CoinsViewOverlay::StartFetching`), everything else serial under `cs_main`.

| | dbcache 450 | 1024 | 8192 |
|---|---|---|---|
| master | 19.9 to 20.3 blocks/s, 259 MiB | 334 MiB | 328 MiB |
| branch, 2 workers | 51.6 to 52.9, 394 to 399 MiB | same | same |
| branch, 16 workers | 138.5 to 152.5, 700 MiB | 792 MiB | 742 MiB |

Master is bound by its message handler (about 80 s of CPU on it). The cache never binds on this chain, so resident memory follows the thread count and not `-dbcache`: at 16 workers 400 to 460 MiB above master, which is the paper sum of 7.6 (470 MiB). The branch is 2.6x master at 2 workers and 7x at 16. This is the master row 7.3 lacks, with its options stated.

**Pruned sync** (`-prune=550`, the tip before the prune rule):

| | rate | resident | coins written | per block |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| master, dbcache 450 | 17.6 | 489 MiB | 2,246,344 in 4 flushes | 2808 |
| branch, dbcache 450 | 120.4 | 690 MiB | 1,160,043 in 23 runs, 232,428 erased | 725 |
| master, dbcache 30, maxmempool 5 | 15.3 | 368 MiB | 5,039,837 in 32 flushes | 6300 |
| branch, dbcache 30, maxmempool 5 | 38.1 | 622 MiB | 8,632,678 in 259 runs, 3,863,106 erased | 5395 |

The low-end corner costs the branch 3.2x in rate and master 1.15x; the branch stays 2.5x master there. With the prune rule the 450 row writes 638,426 coins (4.10).

**The low-end corner profiled** (`-reindex-chainstate`). At `-dbcache=1024` 292.9 blocks/s for 44.9 s of CPU; at `-dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5` 41.5 blocks/s for 176.6 s (connect pool 132.9, effects 36.5). 272 runs occupy 34% of the span (median 46 ms, longest 155 ms); the connector waits behind the effects thread for half of the wall, and the 8 workers are on CPU for 41% of it. Per block a worker's core phase goes from 5.37 to 20.10 ms and its batch phase from 2.02 to 9.80 ms. So the loss has two halves: the pipeline stops for every run, about every 12 blocks, and a block costs a worker 3.2x because lookups fall through to the database. Both belong to the low-end phase (5.2; the spend hints, P7, were built and dropped, 8.1).

**Full reindex** at `-dbcache=1024`: 253.8 to 258.4 blocks/s (noise about 2%), CPU 103 to 107 s (connect 63 to 66, loader 27 to 28, hash pool 9.2, effects 3.0 to 3.2). 16 workers are no faster than 8 (243.8 to 251.0); what bounds it was not measured (5.2).

**Trial 1, the prune rule**: kept; figures in 4.10.

**Trial 2, one byte budget for parsed blocks**: not built. Emulated with `-blockcache` at `-dbcache=450`, 16 workers: 128 MiB 153.4 / 151.5 blocks/s at 705 MiB resident, 56 MiB 151.8 / 140.5 at 631, 16 MiB 146.9 / 140.5 at 587, none 148.2 / 144.2 at 550; pruned at `-dbcache=30`, 8 workers: 128 MiB 38.9 / 39.1 at 603 to 632 MiB, 32 MiB 38.6 / 38.6 at 508 to 522, none 38.2 / 38.2 at 476 to 481. The block cache is worth at most 1.04x here, inside the spread, for about 150 MiB (block files are in the page cache on this box, so a disk-bound machine may differ); a default of an eighth of the coins cache cost 0.96x at 450. Kept: the default of 4.9. Dropped: the counter with its deleter, the refusal at the budget and the wake-up, since they would bound memory the rate does not depend on.

**Trial 3, the hash pool**: removed. The first trial (14 November) compared the heavy-section rate, which a full reindex does not follow, and found nothing. Measured again on the maintainer's "Measure again, if the pool removal is actually better!" (15 November, tip cad649d3ba against the same tip with the removal reverted; chain D, `-dbcache=1024`, BIP30 off, three alternating rounds), the whole run's wall tells what the section rate hides: full reindex 7.4 / 8.1 / 7.2 s at 8 workers and 7.9 / 8.1 / 7.9 s at 16 without the pool against 6.8 / 6.8 / 6.9 and 6.7 / 6.8 / 6.8 s with it (1.11x and 1.18x faster with the pool); the block-file loader's own time 6.50 and 6.35 s without it, 5.28 and 5.28 s with it (1.2x); reindex-chainstate equal. The pool costs about 1 s more CPU and four threads; resident memory shows no consistent difference. A full reindex is bound by the loader on this chain, and the pool hashes the loader's blocks in parallel. A network sync, on "Does it make ibd faster?" (chain D from one local peer, 12 workers, six alternating rounds): 184.3 without the pool against 181.8 with it (means), the pool thread never showing among the busy threads, since a requested block is hashed in its receive task: the pool is off the IBD path whatever the block size. The maintainer does not weigh `-reindex` on its own, so the removal costs IBD nothing, and it went. The plain deserialiser against the helper without its pool is equal within noise on full reindex, reindex-chainstate and sync; one block (413567) hashed on the pool 1.74 ms, serially 2.49 ms, parsed only 1.08 ms, the plain deserialiser 2.33 ms.

**The window estimate's size floor** (15 November; the maintainer asked for low thread counts at `-dbcache=1024`; chain D, BIP30 off, alternating against tip f1d5637789, ratios of means). Serialized size computed on the worker: reindex-chainstate 0.99 / 1.00 at 2 / 4 workers, sync 1.00 / 0.986. Witness bytes counted in the core's input loop: 0.99 / 0.99, sync 0.975 / 0.984. A layout control (a kept, never-called function at the same place): 1.02 / 1.00, sync 1.00 / 0.994. The size known where it is already measured, as on the series: 1.02 / 1.01, sync 1.00 / 1.016. The two variants that walk the block lose about 1% beyond the layout's half percent, likely cache misses on witness memory that nothing else reads after parsing (not measured directly); the known-size variant reads none and costs nothing. Chain D has no witness-heavy blocks, so what the floor changes there is not measured.

**The hold probe** (15 November; chain D, 16 workers, BIP30 off, `-debug=coindb`, two runs each): peak blocks held by queued `BlockConnected` completions, their serialized MiB, undo queued, write-out runs, resident memory. `-reindex-chainstate` at `-dbcache=1024`: 42 / 52 blocks, 12.9 / 11.9 MiB, no undo, no run, 384 to 391 MiB. Sync at 1024: 27 / 41, 24.5 MiB, 1.9 MiB undo, no run, 290 to 298 MiB. `-reindex-chainstate` at `-dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5`: 35 / 31, 5.4 / 4.3 MiB, 58 / 57 runs, 366 to 370 MiB. Sync there: 49 / 59, 18.4 / 28.7 MiB, 2.9 to 3.1 MiB undo, 83 / 78 runs, 507 to 522 MiB. A `-reindex-chainstate` writes no undo, so its backlog is zero and the 64 MiB cap never acts there; the network syncs' backlog stays near 3 MiB. Blocks are held without any write-out run too, a few dozen at any cache size, so the hold looks tied to when completions are applied (in batches), not mainly to runs; not read in the code. The maintainer's mainnet run with the probe (15 November, `-reindex-chainstate` to 975073, `-dbcache=30000`, 32 workers, `-par=16`): peak 39 blocks held (at 338,507), the most bytes 46.9 MiB serialized (31 blocks at 969,555), no undo, no write-out run during the connect; peak VmRSS 29,463 MiB, 13,284 MiB of it in huge pages; 839.5 s against 832.9 s without the probe. Even at two to three times their serialized size the held blocks are about half a percent of the process. Not covered: a network sync with forced runs and undo writes at mainnet scale. Accepted as measured small; the memory claims are closed: the window estimate fixed, the depot peak, the idle bins and the held blocks accepted.

**Trial 4, the effects thread**: kept, no commit. A probe ran the effects elsewhere (three alternating runs each, probe against thread). On the enqueuing thread, so the undo write and the lag run sit inside the `cs_main` hold: network sync at 16 workers 0.82x, full reindex at 8 workers 0.95x. On the connector between two blocks with no lock held: network sync 0.88x, full reindex 0.98x. Seen and not explained: the connect pool's CPU rose by about 10 s on the network sync in both forms while the effects thread itself spends 3.5 to 4 s; resident memory on the full reindex was 540 to 556 MiB without the thread against 665 to 718 MiB with it.

**The second comparison round.** No protocol-level proposal removes a serial limit inside the constraints, and none publishes a parallel figure. Self-reported, each with what it costs here:

- SwiftSync: serial reindex-chainstate 5.28x on a slow VPS, 1.56x on an i9 with NVMe at dbcache 45000, 2.82x / 1.73x on a hard disk at 450 / 4500; Core PR 34004 was closed in May 2026 without a benchmark. Needs a hints file. Taken from it: P7 (built and dropped, 8.1) and P10 (5.4).
- Utreexo: no IBD timing published. UHS: sizes and estimates only. Both need a protocol change.
- Core issue 36476 (`-pruneassumevalid`): a real sync in 3 h 05 to 10 h on four machines, 2.5x on a hard disk; it leaves the serial UTXO section and adds trust (C2, C14).
- Libbitcoin: full validation in 8.5 h / 4.5 h (the author's), 20 h 51 on an i7-8700 with 32 GB where Core 30 took 12 h 07 (Lopp); confirmation is serial; 32 GB minimum.
- Hornet: 15 against 167 minutes with scripts skipped, on 32 cores and 128 GB, the author's; a 48 B index entry a coin and a 134 GiB log. P9 (5.4).
- Core 35295, the parallel prevout fetch master now has: 1.16x at dbcache 1000, nothing at 50000. Core 36000, block read-ahead: 1.30 to 1.53x on reindex-chainstate.

A second independent design from a clean brief: creations published first and unordered, the spend stage gated on a watermark of lower blocks' publications, versioned entries with a compare-and-swap of the spent height, a deferred BIP30 note judged by the committer, bump arenas by 144-block bands (about 90 B a coin by its own estimate), flush cycles to a target height. It agrees on key-range shards, sorted batches, the lag, and the budget inside `-dbcache`; it differs at the centre as the first did: no deferral (P8 in 5.4).

**The series on the harness, `-reindex-chainstate`** (18 November; not a sync, which had no valid figure yet; on the maintainer's "try to reproduce the prior rate of 515 to 540 blocks/s"): the tip 74e8e1db9a from `build-bench` (clang, RelWithDebInfo), chain D, 16 workers on 16 cores, `-dbcache=2000`, `-test=bip30_off`. Warm, 521.4 / 512.3 / 505.9 blocks/s, worker CPU 8.9 to 9.0 s, inside the clang reference of the last harness check of the review rounds (503.0 / 541.8 / 519.0, 4.11's record); ten rounds on a quiet host (foreign load 0.9 to 1.3 cores, the driver's own polling): median 547.9, worker CPU 8.5 to 10.7 s. The rate follows the foreign load run by run (348.9 blocks/s with 7.2 of 16 cores busy elsewhere, 392.0 at 6.0, 443.6 at 4.2, 513.1 to 540.0 at 2.2) while worker CPU stayed at 8.3 to 9.1 s: the series did the same work and waited for cores. Idle sampled just before a run predicts nothing, since the neighbour's load comes in bursts shorter than a run. A harness rate counts only with the foreign load during the run recorded and near the floor, and it says nothing about initial block download. Against master 6f1365e34f (the newest master binary kept) 23.1 blocks/s, against the baseline (6.3: master e211dd0186 with the same `-test=bip30_off`) 27.4: 19.9x between the medians on this harness's `-reindex-chainstate` (544.7 against 27.4). Master has the BIP30 check on (it has no `-test=bip30_off`), a lookup for every created output on its one connect thread, which mainnet master pays only up to height 227,931; it costs master 1.19x here (27.4 against 23.1). So the harness ratio is no prediction for mainnet, where the same tree is 11.26x (the maintainer's 2:10:38 against 11:36.26, 7.1).

**Low core counts and the low-memory budget, `-reindex-chainstate`** (18 November, on the maintainer's "runs with a low thread count ... and interleaved"): chain D, the baseline (6.3) against the tip 74e8e1db9a, both `-test=bip30_off`, three alternating rounds per configuration, host quiet (foreign load 0.5 to 1.8 cores). Each binary pinned with `taskset` and given the thread options it picks by default on that many cores, since pinning does not change the detected core count: the series `-prevoutfetchthreads=max(2,N) -par=N`, the baseline its fixed 8 fetch threads and `-par=N`. Tip / baseline heavy-section blocks/s as medians, and the ratio:

| cores | `-dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5` | `-dbcache=450` | `-dbcache=1024` | `-dbcache=2000` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 27.5 / 15.0, 1.8x | 91.5 / 29.1, 3.1x | 92.9 / 28.7, 3.2x | 92.1 / 29.0, 3.2x |
| 4 | 40.5 / 17.3, 2.3x | 172.2 / 28.1, 6.1x | 176.6 / 27.7, 6.4x | 177.2 / 28.0, 6.3x |
| 8 | 46.8 / 19.2, 2.4x | 324.5 / 27.7, 11.7x | 328.8 / 27.7, 11.9x | 318.6 / 28.2, 11.3x |
| 12 | 47.2 / 19.0, 2.5x | 411.1 / 27.5, 14.9x | 458.1 / 27.3, 16.8x | 455.0 / 27.3, 16.7x |
| 16 | 46.0 / 18.2, 2.5x | 410.1 / 28.4, 14.4x | 550.8 / 26.7, 20.6x | 544.7 / 27.4, 19.9x |

Resident memory at the end, baseline / tip, where read: 2 cores 140 / 164 MiB at 2000 and 247 / 265 at 30/5; 4 cores 148 / 207 and 249 / 303; 16 cores 198 / 400 at 2000.

Readings. The baseline's connect is one thread: about 28 blocks/s at every core count with any large budget, 15 to 19 at 30/5. The tip at 1024 and 2000 scales with the cores through 16 (92, 177, 320 to 330, 455, 545 to 551). At 450 it stops at 12 cores (411, then 410 at 16): the window's byte cap follows the cache (4.5), the false plateau the harness rule of measuring at 1024 exists for. At 30/5 it stops at 8 cores (47) and its ratio is smallest, 1.8x at two cores, where it spends more CPU than the baseline (about 20 s against 16 s a run, both cores busy): the low-end path's own cost (5.2).

What this does not emulate: slower cores, smaller caches and memory systems, storage, and a physical memory limit (no cgroup or root in the container; `-dbcache` sets only the node's own budget, and the block files stay in this machine's page cache).

**The same grid as syncs** (18 November, on the maintainer's "how would you measure this during ibd"): chain D synced by a fresh client from one local serving node, plain master e211dd0186 on cores 14 and 15; the client, baseline (`-test=bip30_off`) or the tip, pinned to N cores with the same thread options as the reindex grid, `-maxtipage=1`, consistency checks off. Three alternating rounds per configuration, foreign load 0.3 to 1.1 cores, no failed run. Heavy-section blocks/s of the sync, tip / baseline medians and the ratio:

| client cores | `-dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5` | `-dbcache=450` | `-dbcache=1024` | `-dbcache=2000` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 23.9 / 14.0, 1.7x | 67.0 / 26.6, 2.5x | 66.4 / 27.7, 2.4x | 66.5 / 26.8, 2.5x |
| 4 | 36.4 / 14.9, 2.4x | 121.3 / 24.4, 5.0x | 119.2 / 24.1, 4.9x | 117.1 / 24.0, 4.9x |
| 8 | 43.3 / 16.7, 2.6x | 178.9 / 24.9, 7.2x | 191.0 / 24.8, 7.7x | 189.8 / 24.4, 7.8x |
| 12 | 42.9 / 15.8, 2.7x | 183.7 / 24.3, 7.6x | 189.2 / 24.1, 7.9x | 188.2 / 24.7, 7.6x |
| 14 | 42.9 / 16.0, 2.7x | 178.6 / 23.7, 7.5x | 179.3 / 23.7, 7.6x | 185.6 / 24.2, 7.7x |

The serving check before it, the tip as client on 12 cores at 1024: one serving node on one core 189.7 blocks/s, on two cores 197.0, two serving nodes on the same two cores 194.9; one node on two cores was taken for the grid. That check does not show that one peer has headroom: in the two-node runs the client took nearly all blocks from one of the two, and the single node's message handler was busy for about 1.6 s of a 2.3 s sync.

Readings. A sync costs the baseline 5 to 15% against its `-reindex-chainstate` (about 24 to 28 blocks/s at the large budgets) and the tip more: 2.5x and 4.9x at two and four cores where the reindex gave 3.2x and 6.3x, since its receiving and storing share the cores with the connect workers. From 8 cores up the tip's sync stops at about 180 to 190 blocks/s at every large budget, where the reindex goes on to 545 at 16 cores. That cap is partly the single master peer (below). At 30/5 the sync stops at about 43 from 8 cores, as the reindex does, at the low-end path (5.2).

**Local mainnet syncs, the series against master** (19 November, on the maintainer's go): a plain master serving node with mainnet to height 250,000 (August 2013, 11 GB of blocks, downloaded once from the internet; `-whitelist=download@127.0.0.1`, no outbound connections, cores 14-15), the client on cores 0-7 from a fresh datadir, `-dbcache=450` with the default mempool, `-minimumchainwork=0` (the server serves headers only along its active chain, below mainnet's minimum work) and `-assumevalid` at block 250,000; the series with `-par=8 -prevoutfetchthreads=8`, master with `-par=8`. The first sync comparison on real blocks without peer luck. (A `-dbcache=100` section was dropped on the maintainer's word.)

| Local mainnet sync to 250,000, `-dbcache=450` | Series | Master | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| v2, wall (two rounds) | 1:55.77 / 1:54.92 | 6:07.18 / 6:08.49 | 3.2x |
| v2, blocks/s over heights 150,000 to 250,000 | 1,190.5 / 1,204.8 | 318.5 / 316.5 | 3.8x |
| v2, user CPU | 511 / 508 s | 635 / 639 s | 0.80x |
| v1 (`-v2transport=0` on both ends, one round), wall | 1:53.82 | 6:15.74 | 3.3x |
| v1, blocks/s over the same range | 1,219.5 | 308.6 | 4.0x |
| v1, user CPU | 487 s | 609 s | 0.80x |

Peak RSS 1,609 to 1,625 MiB for the series against 1,348 to 1,358 for master. The transport hardly matters at 2013's block sizes (the series 1 to 2% faster with v1, 4% less CPU; master not faster). The series' `-reindex-chainstate` of the same blocks took 1:20.87 and 1:13.12 (1,818.2 and 1,923.1 blocks/s over the range, 281 and 264 s user CPU, the second pass with the block files in the page cache), and that comparison was confounded: the sync's client knows headers only to 250,079, so `-assumevalid` checked scripts from block 247,632 on ("block too recent relative to best header", the two weeks' margin, whatever the hash), while the reindex copy carries the whole header chain and checked none.

The gap between sync and reindex, measured cleanly (a throwaway probe build; both runs stop at 247,000, before the sync's script checks): wall 1:37.92 against 1:21.29 (1.20x, the sync's header phase and start included), 1,564.5 against 1,701.8 blocks/s over heights 150,000 to 247,000 (the sync at 92% of the reindex), CPU 475 against 385 s (+24%).

| CPU, to 247,000, `-dbcache=450`, 8 cores | Sync | Reindex |
|---|---|---|
| Receive tasks (parse, check, store) | 106 s, of it 30 s the block write | none |
| Connect tasks | 211 s | 310 s (reads and parses the blocks from disk) |
| Continuations | 22 s (finish 11.6, apply 6.4) | 23 s (12.5, 7.7) |
| Undo writes (on the executor) | 27 s | none (the reindex keeps the undo data on disk) |
| Coins database single reads | 35.0 million, 64 s | 35.2 million, 75 s |
| Coins database batch writes | 2.0 s, 19 batches | 2.0 s, 19 batches |
| Threads: `b-connect` / `b-effects` / `b-net` / `b-msghand` / `b-initload` | 369 / 43 / 37 / 17 / none | 333 / 11 / none / none / 27 |

Parsing moves from the connect task (reindex) to the receive task (sync) rather than costing twice; what the sync does in addition is writing the blocks (30 s) and their undo data (27 s) and the network and message handling (54 s), all work a sync has to do. In the confounded runs the sync's continuations had cost three times the reindex's and the connect tasks 38 s more: script checks of the last 2,368 blocks, not the receive path. What limited the sync's wall time: the connector waited for workers 65% of the time and almost never for blocks (0.1%), with the workers on about four of the eight cores. Early-chain blocks: the shares for today's blocks are not these ones.

What holds the sync back on these blocks (19 November, throwaway probe layers on the tip; local mainnet sync to 247,000, `-dbcache=450`, cores 0-7):
- The connector waits for workers about 56 to 58% of the time, at the lowest block still in its first pass (about 13,000 to 14,500 blocking waits of 2.2 ms each over heights 150,000 to 245,000) far more than at its continuation (5,000 to 6,000 of about 0.8 to 1.0 ms); behind the lowest block only 0.6 higher blocks are complete on average, with 11 to 12.5 of 16 in flight and 6.4 to 7.5 tasks queued in the pool. Finished blocks are not stuck behind a slow one. On chain DX (`-reindex-chainstate`, heavy section, 8 cores) the same probe: 74% waiting for workers, 0.07 blocks complete behind the lowest, 16 of 16 in flight, the queue never empty, the workers at about 6.6 cores: a CPU-bound window. In the mainnet sync the process uses about 4.9 of 8 cores while work is queued, and the mainnet reindex about 4.5 with no receive tasks at all (small early blocks, per-block costs).
- The receive task, accounted for in full: 268 s of wall time for 109 s of CPU, of which 140 s waiting for `cs_main` at five acquisitions a block (three in `ProcessNewBlock`, 71.6 s; two in `ProcessBlockMessage`, the parent lookup and the request bookkeeping, 68.4 s), parse 31.9 s, the block write about 31 s, notify and signal 9.9 s, `CheckBlock`, the mutation check, the contextual checks and the finish 14.2 s, peer checks and bookkeeping 1.8 s.
- Capping the receive tasks (the default being the pool's workers less one, 7) makes the sync slower, steeply: 1,539.7 blocks/s over 150,000 to 247,000 at 7, 1,492.3 at 4, 1,197.5 at 2, 808.3 at 1. Receiving is on the critical path; the receive tasks' lock waits do not starve the connect tasks.
- Judged on heavy blocks (chain DX sync, three master peers, 8 cores, `-dbcache=1024`): 234.7 blocks/s; receive tasks 29.3 s of wall for 22.1 s of CPU, of which waiting for `cs_main` 4.8 s (17%), about 0.76 ms a block against about 0.57 ms on the early mainnet blocks, while the work per block is about eight times as much. The connector waited for workers 79% of the time with only 7.9 of 16 window places filled: the blocks come no faster, the sync is at the v2 network thread's ceiling (about 245). So the receive path's `cs_main` waits are an early-chain effect (many tiny blocks, a fixed cost a block), not worth a change for today's blocks; merging two acquisitions would save about 1 s in 29.
- Built anyway as step A of a trial on the maintainer's word: `ProcessBlockMessage`'s two acquisitions move into `HandOffBlock()`'s hold on the message handler. No gain: local mainnet syncs to 247,000 at 450 MiB 1:30.24 / 1:29.87 against the tip's 1:29.26 / 1:30.96, chain DX syncs 232.9 against 236.1 blocks/s (medians of three). The receive tasks' `cs_main` waits overlap other work; the critical path is the workers' first passes and the connector. Step B (merging the header and storage holds) not built. Without the probe the tip syncs these blocks in about 1:30, about 1,690 blocks/s, so the probe layers cost about 10% and the sync's gap to the reindex (1,818 to 1,923) is smaller than the probe figures above.

Chain DX's top ages: a master client asks only one peer for headers until its best header is under a day old (`net_processing.cpp`, "Only actively request headers from a single peer, unless we're close to today"), so once the top is a day old, syncs fall back to one peer. One empty block mined with the day's time (`cpu/fresh_top.sh`; height 6,382, 0d9906a26b8e) was added to the three serving copies, the reindex datadir and the source copy, and `tip.txt` updated; the measured range (2,781 to 4,281) is unchanged, and the first sync after it took three peers evenly at 231.9 blocks/s (8 cores, 450). The script adds another block when the top ages again.

**One peer against two, sync** (18 November, on the maintainer's "take the fastest configuration ... does it improve if you run two peers"): the tip as client on 8 cores at `-dbcache=1024`, one serving node (cores 14 and 15) against two on cores of their own, alternating, three rounds, block bytes per peer read from `getpeerinfo`. On chain D the client took every block from one peer and the rates were equal (one node 183.1 / 177.5 / 180.2, two 181.4 / 180.9 / 180.6): master's net processing syncs headers from a single peer unless the best header is under 24 hours old, and learns another peer's best block only from headers or an announcement, which a static serving node never sends. Chain D's blocks are days old, so a second peer is never asked; any multi-peer harness run needs a chain whose top is under a day old, and the one-, two- and four-peer readings of 7.5 may share the artifact (not checked). On chain DX, built the same day, both peers served (978 / 975, 982 / 971, 977 / 977 MiB): one node 163.9 / 169.9 / 171.8 blocks/s, two 201.4 / 191.6 / 188.3, medians 169.9 and 191.6, 1.13x. The single node's message handler used 9.5 to 9.8 s of CPU in a sync of 11.5 to 12.2 s, the two nodes' 5.2 to 5.4 s each. Reading: one master peer is near its limit at this rate and caps the harness sync by about 13%; with two the client syncs at about 190, against about 320 for its `-reindex-chainstate` at 8 cores, and the next limit is in the client (below).

**What holds the harness sync back** (18 November, on the maintainer's "find it out"): chain DX, the tip as client on 8 cores at `-dbcache=1024`, measured step by step.
- Same-chain reference: chain DX's `-reindex-chainstate` at those settings 327.7 / 319.2 / 312.6 blocks/s for about 44 s of CPU a run; the two-peer sync about 190 to 200 for 61 to 65 s. A sync makes the client do about 45% more work on the same blocks.
- Peers: three serving nodes (192.2 / 201.6) give what two give, each handler a third busy: the serving side has room.
- The network thread: with v2 transport it uses 0.79 to 0.81 of a core (the decryption of about 260 MB/s); with the client on v1 (`-v2transport=0`) 0.31, and the sync rises only to 216.6 / 219.5 (1.09x). Encryption costs about half a core of that thread, but the thread is not the limit.
- Disk: thread states sampled every 10 ms show almost no uninterruptible waits, 2% on the effects thread only.
- Cores: the process uses 6.5 of its 8 cores; each connect worker is on a CPU about 0.71 of the time, waits for a core 0.09 and sleeps 0.20; the connector 0.06, 0.28 and 0.66; the scheduler thread and the message handler hardly run but wait for a core 0.46 and 0.31. Every peer stays at 16 to 20 blocks in flight in every sample, and more peers raise the blocks in flight (about 36 to 54) without raising the rate: received blocks queue inside the client.
- Locks: a probe build of the tip with `-DDEBUG_LOCKCONTENTION` and `-debug=lock`, three peers, v1, 223.2 blocks/s under the probe. Per second of sync the connect threads wait 1.2 s on `cs_main`, the message handler 0.19 s; the store's shard locks 0.09 s. By site: the receive task's look-up of the previous block's index for the mutation check 0.55 s; accepting the header 0.21 s; the connector's publication hold (with the mempool lock) 0.17 s; `SendMessages` 0.15 s; preparing the storage 0.11 s; after the write 0.08 s; `IsBlockRequested` 0.08 s. A received block takes `cs_main` about five times in its receive task, and a contended wait lasts about 9 ms on average even where the hold itself is a look-up, so some holder keeps the lock for milliseconds.

That reading named the waiters, not the cause; the probes below found it.

- Probe A (the receive task's previous-block look-up taken out of its own hold, moved into the hold the task takes next; regtest only): 224.2 blocks/s against 222.4 for the tip, three alternating rounds. The largest wait was a symptom; its waiting moves to the next acquisition.
- Probe B (every outermost `cs_main` hold timed by site, and the connector's waits; over the heavy blocks): the long holder is `PrepareAcceptBlockStorage()`, once per received block, 0.87 ms on average and up to 190 ms, 0.23 s per second of sync; the connector's publication hold 0.64 ms on average, 0.06 s per second; the previous-block look-up 2 us. The connector waits 0.66 s per second for the worker of the lowest block in flight, 0.16 s for `cs_main` at its publication hold, 0.09 s for the validation callback queue, nothing for hand-offs, the undo backlog or pinned blocks.
- Under the lock, `PrepareAcceptBlockStorage()` claims the block's place through `AllocateBlockPos()` and `FindNextBlockPos()`: the block's serialized size, the file's growth in 16 MiB steps, and at each move to a new block file `FlushBlockFile(..., fFinalize=true)`, a sync of the 128 MiB file. This is master's code, unchanged; on master one thread runs it and no other waits behind it.
- Probe C (probe B with the file-switch flush skipped; the same binary alternating, three rounds): 214.0 / 221.1 / 227.4 blocks/s with the flush, 253.8 / 259.8 / 254.1 without, medians 221.1 and 254.1, 1.15x. Without it the storage hold is 0.22 ms on average, 6 ms at most, 0.07 s per second, and the connector's wait for `cs_main` 0.05 s per second. Per-thread sampling then shows the client at 7.6 of its 8 cores, each worker on a CPU 0.81 to 0.86 of the time and the pool 1.25 cores ready without one.

Reading, in order of what binds: one master peer caps the harness sync by about 13% (two or more peers, chain DX); the block-file flush made under `cs_main` when the write position moves to a new file holds the lock for up to 190 ms, which stalls every receive task and the connector, about 15%; with both out of the way the client is CPU-bound, a sync costing about a quarter more CPU per block than the `-reindex-chainstate` of the same chain (receiving, checking and writing the blocks, the undo data); the v2 transport's decryption takes about half a core of the network thread (1.09x when the client uses v1). A `-reindex-chainstate` writes no blocks, so it moves to no new block file and pays none of the flushes.

**The block-file trim: the left block file trimmed, not synced** (18 November, on the maintainer's "execute on the full thing"). The series needs no sync at the switch: every block file written since its last sync stays marked, a write-out run's batch syncs all of them on the executor before it commits (`SyncBatchFiles()`), a synchronous write syncs them first (`SyncWrittenFiles()`), and `CommitBlockIndexBatch()` asserts that a batch owes none (no other production writer of block positions exists). Master syncs at the switch because its index write syncs only the current file. So `FindNextBlockPos()` trims the left file (`FlatFileSeq::Truncate()`; since the I/O work the trim is queued, `QueueBlockFileTrim()` and `UnpinBlockFile()`, 4.14), and its undo file where that is finished, without a sync; blocks written since the last index write may be lost in a crash and are downloaded again. Unit case `blockmanager_left_file_synced_by_the_index_batch` (the left file trimmed and unsynced, the next batch owes and makes its sync, then commits; it fails on the old code, whose switch sync took the file off the list). Measured, chain DX synced from three local peers, the client on 8 cores at `-dbcache=1024`, v2 transport, three alternating rounds: baseline 22.0, the tip 209.9, the commit 238.0 blocks/s (medians; 1.13x over the tip, 10.8x the baseline where the tip was 9.5x), client CPU unchanged at about 60 s a run. Chain D's `-reindex-chainstate`, which writes no blocks, unchanged.

**What limits the harness sync after the block-file trim: the network thread** (18 November, on the maintainer's "what is still holding up the harness sync now"): chain DX from three local peers, v2 transport, `-dbcache=1024`, per-thread sampling over the heavy blocks. On 8 cores: 241.1 blocks/s, the process at 7.61 of 8 cores, the workers on a CPU 0.74 to 0.79 of the time with 1.62 cores ready without one, the network thread at 0.94 of a core. On 12 cores: median 240.6, the network thread at 0.99 of a core, the workers on a CPU 0.57 to 0.60 of the time and almost nothing ready without a core.
