The network thread's load follows the rate with v2 (0.79 of a core at 200 blocks/s, 0.94 at 240, about 4 ms of a core per heavy block; chain DX's heavy blocks carry about 1.135 MB each, so about 273 MB/s, 2.2 Gbit/s, at the peak); with v1 it was 0.31 at 218 blocks/s. Reading: the single thread that reads and decrypts every peer's traffic caps this harness sync at about 245 blocks/s on this machine; on 8 cores the client is also out of cores. This is master's design, one socket thread for every peer with v2 on by default, and at that thread's limit, about 273 MB/s of decrypted block data, a real network link below about 2.2 Gbit/s binds first. Why the thread is that slow: `bench_bitcoin` on one core gives `FSCHACHA20POLY1305_1MB` (the v2 transport's cipher) 392 MB/s, `CHACHA20_1MB` 560 MB/s, `POLY1305_1MB` 1,311 MB/s; the tree has only the portable C++ ChaCha20, no SIMD path. Decrypting a 1.135 MB block takes about 2.9 ms and the thread's other work about 1.4 ms a block (its load with v1), together the 4.1 ms measured: about 70% of the network thread is decryption. Ways past it, none built: a faster cipher; decrypting block payloads on the receive tasks (a v2 packet's payload key and nonce follow from its position, so only the framing need stay on the network thread); more socket threads. On the harness core (a Neoverse-N1 with 128-bit NEON) OpenSSL 3.5.5's optimised code gives ChaCha20-Poly1305 863 to 874 MB/s and ChaCha20 1,248 MB/s, 2.2x Core's 392 and 560; with its SIMD switched off (`OPENSSL_armcap=0`) 365 and 533 MB/s, as fast as Core's portable code, so the whole difference is the NEON path (Core's `ChaCha20Aligned::Keystream()` computes one 64-byte block at a time in scalar registers). At OpenSSL's speed a heavy block would take about 1.3 ms to decrypt, the network thread about 2.7 ms a block, a ceiling near 370 blocks/s (about 3.3 Gbit/s) on one thread; inferred, not measured. On the maintainer's x86 machine the same portable code runs; its ceiling there is the same benchmark's figure.

**The sync grid with the current code** (18 November, on the maintainer's "measure the full grid again"): chain DX synced from three local serving nodes (plain master on cores 14 and 15), the tree of 7c789d7356 against the baseline, the client pinned to N cores with the thread options it picks by default there, v2 transport. Per configuration three runs of the current code and one of the baseline between them (the baseline varied under 2% before); foreign load at most 1.0 core. Heavy-section blocks/s of the sync, current code / baseline medians and the ratio:

| client cores | `-dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5` | `-dbcache=450` | `-dbcache=1024` | `-dbcache=2000` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 22.9 / 12.3, 1.9x | 61.8 / 21.8, 2.8x | 62.0 / 22.3, 2.8x | 62.4 / 22.2, 2.8x |
| 4 | 35.3 / 13.3, 2.7x | 133.1 / 21.5, 6.2x | 131.2 / 22.2, 5.9x | 131.5 / 22.5, 5.8x |
| 8 | 42.4 / 15.1, 2.8x | 226.0 / 22.0, 10.3x | 237.7 / 22.0, 10.8x | 238.4 / 22.2, 10.7x |
| 12 | 43.6 / 15.0, 2.9x | 247.4 / 21.9, 11.3x | 247.1 / 22.1, 11.2x | 247.6 / 21.9, 11.3x |
| 14 | 43.7 / 15.3, 2.9x | 243.9 / 21.3, 11.5x | 246.0 / 21.9, 11.2x | 246.8 / 21.9, 11.3x |

Readings. The three runs of the current code agree within about 2% everywhere. The baseline syncs at about 22 blocks/s at every core count with a large budget and 12 to 15 at 30/5. The current code stops at about 245 blocks/s from 8 or 12 cores up at every large budget, the network thread's ceiling of the paragraphs above (about 280 MB/s of block data, about 72% of the v2 cipher's 392 MB/s on one core, the rest the thread's other work). At 30/5 it stops at about 43 from 8 cores, the low-end path (5.2). Unlike the single-peer grid on chain D, 450 no longer falls behind the larger budgets beyond 8 cores, since the network thread binds first.

v1 against v2 with the current code (on the maintainer's question, same chain, peers and serving cores, 14 client cores, `-dbcache=1024`, three alternating rounds): v2 249.7 / 246.3 / 242.0, v1 (`-v2transport=0` on the client) 379.0 / 378.0 / 377.4 blocks/s, medians 246.3 and 378.0, 1.53x; client CPU about 67 s against 61 s a run, the serving nodes' 11 s against 5 s (they no longer encrypt), resident memory about 350 against about 520 MiB (more blocks in flight and in the window). A sampled v1 run, 384.2 blocks/s: the network thread at 0.57 of a core, the process at 12.29 of 14 cores, each connect worker on a CPU 0.77 to 0.80 of the time with 1.34 cores ready without one, the effects thread 0.73. Reading: the v2 ceiling of about 245 blocks/s is the network thread decrypting, now measured and not only inferred; with v1 the client is CPU-bound at about 32 ms of core time per heavy block, and the sync scales with cores again; 378 blocks/s is about 430 MB/s of block data, 3.4 Gbit/s.

**Pruned syncs** (19 November, on the maintainer's go): chain DX with `-prune=550` on the client (normal 128 MiB block files, no `-fastprune`), the setup of the sync grid above, the current code against the baseline, three runs of the current code and one of the baseline per configuration. Every run pruned 11 times and ended at 528 to 529 MiB of block data; foreign load at most 1.0 core. The first measurement showed what pruning cost the series: 20% at 4 cores (105.2 against 131.2 unpruned), 36% at 8 (152.9 against 237.7), 27% at 14 (179.8 against 246.0), against 15 to 17% for the baseline at every count (about 18.5 against 22), at the low budget 8%, 9% and 9% against 3%, 2% and 7%. Its client CPU barely rose with pruning (about 67 s against 60 s a run at 8 cores, 1024), so the loss was waiting rather than work. The cause, measured with hold timing and a step timer in the prune path (8 cores, `-dbcache=1024`): the pruning check is requested at every allocation of block or undo space (`FindNextBlockPos()`, `FindUndoPos()`), every few heavy blocks, and each check began with `SettleWriteOut()` under `cs_main`, the effects queue drained also below the prune target where nothing is chosen (488 checks over 0.5 ms in a 10 s span, 4.8 s in all; master's check below the target costs nothing); and at each of the 11 prune events the run pruning needed (to bring the written head up to the prune height, C5, D1) was waited for under the lock, 87 to 415 ms. The connector's publication hold was 9.31 ms on average and 514 ms at most (0.48 s per second of sync), against 0.62 ms, 6.6 ms and 0.063 s unpruned. A first fix (the run waited for after the hold only) moved the wait into a receive task's acceptance flush, which met the run in flight at the same gate, and left the rate unchanged: the waits below the target were the larger part. The pruning waits now settle only when files will be chosen, defer the prune run in the window to the connector's wait after the hold, and have a check leave the flag set while a run is in flight. Left under the lock at each event (probe on the fix): the checks at the target (1.0 s over the span), the block-index write with its syncs (466 ms over the 11 events) and the unlink (311 ms), all of which master also does under `cs_main`. Pruned sync at `-dbcache=1024` and `-dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5`, medians, before (the pruning waits) and after; unpruned from the sync grid:

| Configuration | Series pruned after (before) | Unpruned | Cost after (before) | Baseline pruned | Baseline cost | Ratio after (before) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 cores, 1024 | 114.2 (105.2) | 131.2 | -13% (-20%) | 18.6 | -16% | 6.1x (5.7x) |
| 4 cores, 30/5 | 33.0 (32.4) | 35.3 | -7% (-8%) | 13.0 | -2% | 2.5x (2.5x) |
| 8 cores, 1024 | 176.6 (152.9) | 237.7 | -26% (-36%) | 18.3 | -17% | 9.7x (8.2x) |
| 8 cores, 30/5 | 39.7 (38.5) | 42.4 | -6% (-9%) | 14.4 | -5% | 2.8x (2.6x) |
| 14 cores, 1024 | 219.4 (179.8) | 246.0 | -11% (-27%) | 17.9 | -18% | 12.3x (9.8x) |
| 14 cores, 30/5 | 40.9 (39.9) | 43.7 | -6% (-9%) | 14.4 | -6% | 2.8x (2.8x) |

Pruning now costs the series less than the baseline at the large budget at 4 and 14 cores and more at 8 (26% against 17%), and about as much as the baseline at the low one. One run with `-checkblockindex=1` was clean. Peak RSS at 8 cores 508 to 549 MiB against 374 to 383 before (the undo queue is no longer drained at every check). Which parts of the pruning waits are needed (builds from the tip with parts removed, interleaved, three rounds, pruned, 1024, medians at 8 / 14 cores): the settle only at the target alone 174.3 / 193.9; with the deferred run but without `m_prune_at_periodic_write` 178.7 / 191.7 (the deferral does not take effect); with both 181.4 / 222.0; the full commit, which also started no memory run in a flush that prunes in the hold, 180.1 / 217.8. That fourth part was taken out: the commit is the 181.4 / 222.0 build.

Pruned at `-dbcache=450`, the realistic low end (the maintainer, 19 November: 450 is the low end that counts, at 30 the series only has to be no slower than the baseline):

| Cores | Series pruned, three runs, median | Unpruned | Cost | Baseline pruned | Baseline unpruned | Baseline cost | Ratio pruned (unpruned) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | 113.8 / 114.4 / 117.0, 114.4 | 133.1 | -14% | 18.5 | 21.5 | -14% | 6.2x (6.2x) |
| 8 | 178.4 / 160.4 / 160.1, 160.4 | 226.0 | -29% | 18.0 | 22.0 | -18% | 8.9x (10.3x) |
| 14 | 175.7 / 205.2 / 193.1, 193.1 | 243.9 | -21% | 18.8 | 21.3 | -12% | 10.3x (11.5x) |

The series' runs at 8 and 14 cores spread wider than at 1024 (11% and 16%). Client CPU per run 58 to 84 s against the baseline's 142 to 148 s; peak RSS 375 to 611 MiB against 309 to 327 MiB (the baseline's cache is emptied at each prune event's flush). Pruning costs the series as much as the baseline at 4 cores and more at 8 and 14; what holds `cs_main` at 450 was not sampled. Hypothesis, untested (left on the maintainer's word: pruning is fine for now): at 450 memory runs are frequent, and a flush that unlinks pruned files waits under `cs_main` for a memory run it starts (`|| fFlushForPrune` in the lag path), the collision the removed fourth part of the pruning waits avoided, measured only at 1024. At 30 the series is 1.9x to 2.9x the baseline in syncs at every core count, 2 cores included (22.9 against 12.3 blocks/s, client CPU 154 against 220 s), so the floor holds.

A pruned mainnet IBD on the harness host (19 November, on the maintainer's go; an end-to-end check, not a performance figure): the tip 343b027776, `-prune=20000 -par=16 -prevoutfetchthreads=16 -stopatheight=975073 -disablewallet`, 16 cores (Neoverse-N1, no SMT), 30.5 GiB RAM, a QEMU virtual disk, about ten peers on v2. Heights 0 to 856,353 with `-dbcache=16000` in 1:35:22 (peak RSS 21.1 GB), then resumed on the same datadir with `-dbcache=6000` to 975,073 in 0:53:04 (20.8 GB): 2:28:26 in all, a clean stop, no warning but the absent Tor control port. A first attempt with `-prune=550` was stopped at about height 375,000 and deleted on the maintainer's word: at that target every prune event forces a write-out to a few hundred blocks below the tip, and the single write-out executor ran saturated. What set the pace: the peers (every request slot full, two of nine delivering 60%) to about height 450,000; the network thread's v2 decryption (92% of a core at 213 MB/s) and the prune events (master's IBD buffer prunes to just below the tip, so each event writes out most of the cache and the connector waits for it, about a minute at a time) to about 830,000; then disk reads. With the 16000 cache only about 6.7 GB of page cache was left for a 13 GB chainstate, the page cache thrashed (700 MB/s read at 11,500 reads/s for about 30 MB/s of blocks) and the rate fell to 8.6 blocks/s; at 6000 the reads fell to 1.6 MB/s and the rate rose to about 78, until the 2024-2025 blocks took the disk to its random-read limit (34,100 reads/s at about 9 KB, 100% busy) at about 25 blocks/s. Nothing in it points at the series: master with the same cache meets the same page cache and disk limits. What it shows:
- The series syncs a pruned node through the whole chain, the 2023 to 2025 blocks included, with a clean stop and no fault, in absolute terms fast for the hardware (about 700 GB in 2:28, about 80 MB/s on average, at the network thread's v2 ceiling in its good stretches). It does not show the series' share of that speed: there was no master run on the same host, and with real peers one would be blurred by peer luck; a ratio needs the maintainer's machine or a harness run.
- The cache must leave the chainstate room in the page cache. On disk the UTXO set is compact (13 GB here at 975,073); in the coins cache each coin takes a larger structure, so a page cache that holds the chainstate files covers more coins per GB than the coins cache. The rule of thumb: leave the chainstate's size on disk, plus room for the block files the window reads back, in the page cache, and give the rest to `-dbcache`. Not series-specific; the exact break-even point was not tested.
- At a small prune target pruning becomes a continuous write-out. The series deletes a file only below a coins head on disk (C5, D1), so at `-prune=550` every prune event forced a run to a few hundred blocks below the tip, about 200 write-out lines a minute, with the connect workers idle. At 20 GB the events were rare but large.
- Master's IBD prune buffer turns the bounded prune run into a whole-cache write. During IBD `FindFilesToPrune` adds 1 MB per remaining block to its buffer, so each event prunes to just below the tip; master flushes its whole cache at every prune event whatever it deletes, but in the series a prune run writes only the changes at or below the height pruned to, so pruning by need would write only the oldest changes per event. Spend ages (7.5: 79% of coins spent within about 144 blocks) suggest the total written would change little; the gain would be many short stalls instead of few long ones. Not built, not measured.
- The stall is the connector's wait for the run, not the write itself. A run started in the publication hold is waited for before the next block is published (the store's run must not see newly confirmed changes), so each prune event paused publication for about a minute from 2018 on. Pruning itself needs only that the files wait for the run (the pruning waits already choose them later). Letting runs overlap publication (5.2, the low-end lever) would remove these stalls and the memory runs' alike, but 4.4 records that a run overlapping publication is wrong as such, and that the simple rule (cap the settled height below any dirty entry changed during the run) would hold the written height almost still, since published blocks spend coins of every age. A prune run would need its own form of it: a design question for the low-end phase.
- The peers limit early on through window and round trip: with every request slot full (130 to 155 blocks in flight), two peers with 25 and 114 ms pings delivered 60% of the data and peers with 0.4 to 2 s pings 4 to 29 MB/s each. A larger request window for peers that deliver quickly, beside the existing rule for slow ones, would be the receive-side lever; noted, not pursued. v2 decryption on the single network thread binds on a real network too (92% of a core at 213 MB/s). `getpeerinfo` did not answer within 30 s under IBD load, once; worth a look only if it recurs.
- HDD setups (reasoning, untested): the series' parallel coin reads across a window give a disk a deep queue it can reorder, the syncs left `cs_main`, and the 50% write-out share wrote 26% fewer changes than master's write-everything in the maintainer's runs of 16 November (12 runs against 9, the connector stopped 28.0 against 39.4 s). The connector's wait for each run grows with disk latency and is the weak point there, a pruned HDD node with a small cache the worst case. The gain over master is expected to be real but smaller than on an SSD. Settled only by a `-reindex-chainstate` with the chainstate on an HDD, series against master, and the share curve repeated there.

**The harness sync's limits, in the order they bind** (summary of the paragraphs above, 18 November; chain DX, three local peers unless noted, the current code):

| # | Limit | Status | Binds at | Evidence | Way past |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | one master peer | removed with two or more peers | about 170 blocks/s, 8 cores | its message handler about 80% busy; two peers 191.6 against 169.9 (1.13x); three no better than two | two or more peers, a chain top under a day old on the harness |
| 2 | block-file sync under `cs_main` at a new block file (master's code) | fixed, the block-file trim | about 210 blocks/s, 8 cores | holds up to 190 ms; probe C 221 to 254; the commit 209.9 to 238.0 (1.13x) | trim, the next index write syncs |
| 3 | v2 decryption on the one network thread | the limit with v2 | about 245 blocks/s from 8 cores, every large budget (about 280 MB/s) | the thread at 0.94 to 0.99 of a core, workers idle; the cipher 392 MB/s a core; v1 378.0 against 246.3 (1.53x) | a faster cipher (OpenSSL's 874 MB/s here), decryption spread over peers; not built |
| 4 | the client's cores | the limit with v1 | about 380 blocks/s at 14 cores (about 430 MB/s) | 12.3 of 14 cores, workers ready without a core; about 32 ms of core time a heavy block | more cores, cheaper work per block |
| 5 | the effects thread (undo writes, runs) | next serial limit, projected | about 520 blocks/s | 0.73 of a core at 384 blocks/s | not examined |
| 6 | the network thread without decryption (v1) | projected | about 670 blocks/s | 0.57 of a core at 384 blocks/s | socket reading over more threads |
| 7 | the connector, `cs_main` contention | not near at these rates | not measured above about 400 | the connector waits for workers | watch at higher rates |
| | `-dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5` | separate plateau | about 43 blocks/s from 8 cores (2.9x master) | flat from 8 to 14 cores | the low-end path (5.2) |

Ruled out on the way: disk waits (about 0%), the serving side (spare capacity), the receive-side `cs_main` look-ups (probe A, no change). Master syncs at about 22 blocks/s at every core count. Rows 5 and 6 are projections from loads measured at one rate.

**The compact entry** (19 November, on the maintainer's go, measured on chain DX only by his word; 8 cores; logs in the job's temporary directory). Step 1, a `StoredCoin` of 48 bytes with scripts inline up to 35 bytes (`cpu/compact_ab.sh`): 137.4 against 145.5 bytes per coin at height 4,281 (-5.6%; chain DX's coins mostly have short scripts); `-reindex-chainstate` at 1024 319.6 against 321.3 blocks/s (medians of three); sync from three peers at 1024 233.8 to 237.2 against 234.7 to 240.2; at `-dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5` 42.1 / 42.4 / 42.5 against 41.7 / 42.4 / 41.3 blocks/s, write-out runs 147 / 144 / 148 against 157 / 157 / 156. Step 2, 32-bit cell numbers for the chain link, the bucket array and the list links of that day, the cell 104 bytes (120 before) and a bucket 4 bytes (`cpu/compact_ab2.sh`): 112.9 against 145.5 bytes per coin (-22%); with the region table behind a function-local static `-reindex-chainstate` at 1024 was 4.3% slower (315.0 against 329.3) and syncs at 1024 3 to 8% slower, with the constant-initialized table 320.3 against 324.2 (step 1 318.4) and syncs 236.8 / 237.0 / 229.8 against 240.9 / 235.1 / 232.6, equal within the noise; at `-dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5` (before the table fix) 43.5 / 44.2 / 43.3 against 42.8 / 41.7 / 42.5 blocks/s, write-out runs 121 against 155 (-22%), client CPU 178 against 192 s (-7%). A third step, the heights and flags in one word, saved nothing on that cell (padding); the flags later went into the filed height's word with the 44-byte coin (below). On the series on the maintainer's word, the messages without the figures and without the `-dbcache` clamp for the 256 GiB node limit; the maintainer's mainnet run is 11:49.68 (7.1).

**The store's cell and two removals on the harness** (25 and 26 November; chain DX, binaries from `build-bench`, three alternating rounds each, UTXO hash as expected in every case; none measured on mainnet unless 7.1 says so):
- Without the thread that made the next node region ready and the three store ask-aheads (8.1), `-reindex-chainstate`, BIP30 off, 16 workers, `cpu/prune_ab.sh`, foreign load 5.6 to 8.2, 641e792ad1 (with both) / 0787e0510e (without): 628.6, 657.6, 665.8 / 657.6, 679.6, 649.7 blocks/s at `-dbcache=1024` (CPU 37.7 to 39.4 / 37.2 to 37.8 s), 586.5, 580.9, 588.7 / 586.1, 602.7, 571.7 at 450 (CPU 37.9 to 38.4 / 37.8 to 39.2 s): equal within the spread; UTXO hash d4ba681a7662. The mainnet run's 2.1% more user CPU without the ask-aheads (9:56.80, 7.1) does not show on chain DX, whose store of about 20 MiB leaves the prefetches little to save.
- The cell from 104 to 96 bytes, in two commits on the maintainer's word ("work on further reducing the memory footprint"; 38385b6004; chain DX's coins P2WSH, 246,383 at the tip): the entry's flags in its filed height's word, with the node allocator's free lists linked by node number, then a 44-byte `StoredCoin`. 112.8, 108.5 and 104.7 bytes a coin for the tree before, after the first and after the second commit (the cache at the tip 26.5, 25.5 and 24.6 MiB); rates 0.989x and 0.998x for the first at `-dbcache=1024` and 450, 0.982x and 0.995x for the second, inside the spread, level at 30.
- `PrefetchNode()` asking for two lines of a 96-byte cell where it asked for three (2d478529df, an outside reviewer's point, Neon, 26 November): 1.004x and 0.991x at `-dbcache=1024` and 450, level at 30. Since the store lost its entry lists the cell is 88 bytes and the prefetch asks for every line it may lie on, up to three (4.2).
- The store without entry lists: 5.8, and 7.1 for the maintainer's runs.

### 7.9 The memory profile of 2026-11-18 (the maintainer's machine, the series at 74e8e1db9a)

Two collections with `/work/patches/utxo-partial-order-memprofile.sh` on a `-reindex-chainstate` run (16 cores, 32 threads, `-dbcache=30000 -prevoutfetchthreads=32`), sampled late in the run for ten seconds: one on glibc (at height 841,015), one with jemalloc preloaded (at height 856,366). The preload is a measuring aid only; no allocator swap goes on the series (section 2), and the run time with it is not recorded on the maintainer's word. The two samples are 15,000 blocks apart, so small differences between them mean nothing. `-par` differed between the runs and is irrelevant below assumevalid.

Readings:

- The processors are fully occupied: all 32 connect workers carry about the same share. The run is memory-bound: 0.55 instructions per cycle on glibc, 0.65 with jemalloc, against 2.26 measured above assumevalid.
- The allocator is about 23% of CPU on glibc (frees about 17 points, mostly `_int_free_merge_chunk`) and about 3.4% with jemalloc. Instructions in ten seconds rose from 766 to 898 billion, so removing the allocator's cost altogether is worth about 1.09x: the ceiling for allocation work in code. CPU saved converts at about half, the rest is absorbed by the store's loads. The call stacks stop at the libc boundary, so the allocating call sites are not identified.
- The coin hash (`SaltedCoinsCacheHasher`) is 11 to 12% of CPU in both runs, pure computation. `memset` 2 to 3%, `SerializeTransaction<SizeComputer>` 2 to 3%, `_copy_to_iter` 1.6 to 2.6%.
- Cache misses are the same in both runs (6.4 billion in ten seconds) although everything else changed.
- Far loads (c2c header, jemalloc run): of the loads that leave a core's own caches, 66% are served from main memory, 18% from a line modified in another core's cache, 17% from the shared L3. The table of contended lines came out empty.
- Load latency by source line (jemalloc run): about 32% of the weight sits on the store's prefetch instructions (`Map::Ahead()`: 16.1% in the `ApplyBatch()` batch, 11.1% in `FetchFor()`, 4.7% the write prefetch of list neighbours) and about 3% on the probes that follow them (the key compare in `find()` inside `ApplyBatch()` is 0.14%). The prefetches are timely: neither late nor missing.
- The other waits are scattered: the block-file copy out of the page cache 19.4% in many short loads; `ConfirmBlock()` reading the change record other workers wrote 4.8%; `BlockApplyView::Prefetch()` collecting outpoints 4.1%; `ApplyBatch()` outside the batch 6.8%; `AnnounceCreates()` about 2.8%; `ApplyChanges()` 1.5%; `BlockMerkleRoot()` 1.3%. No single large target.

Read later from the same collections and runs:

- Settled 2026-11-19 from the same two collections' CPU recordings (perf record, cycles, 5 s): the two batch lambdas carry 19.9% and 13.1% of the load-latency weight on glibc but 1.98% and 1.67% of the cycles, and 20.9% and 11.9% against 3.87% and 3.42% with jemalloc. A thread waiting on a load accrues cycle samples there, so the weight on the prefetch lines is their fill latency, overlapped, not waiting: the store's misses are hidden. The cycles go to computation and many small items instead (the coin hash 11.6%, `BlockApplyView::ApplyChanges()` 5.2%, SHA-256 3.6%, `memset` 3.1%, frees 2.8%, the kernel's block-file copy 2.6%, jemalloc collection).
- Whether 32 threads saturate the memory system: the maintainer's runs of 18 November with 16 and 24 connect threads (7.1) answer as far as one machine can: 12:27.55 and 11:46.05 against 11:36.26 at 32, so the threads beyond 16 buy 7% of wall time for 1.79x the CPU time, and those beyond 24 1.4%. The processor has 16 cores and 32 hardware threads, so the result does not separate the memory system from a core's shared resources (two workers per core above 16); either way the parallelism past one worker per core is nearly spent there, and the remaining time is the work per coin. A rough bound: 6.4 billion cache misses in 10 s, about 41 GB/s if each is a 64-byte line, a large share of dual-channel DDR5 in practice but not clearly its limit (the counter's meaning on this processor and the write traffic not checked).
- Why a prefetch weighs ten to fourteen times a block-copy load. Either the memory system is saturated by 32 threads (which would also explain the equal miss counts, and harness gains that ran flat on this machine), or a core queues the burst `PrefetchAll()` issues behind its limit of outstanding misses, which is harmless while the probes arrive after the data. The 16-thread run (above) does not tell the two apart, since two hardware threads share a core's miss capacity; open.

Candidates named then, settled since: a cheaper coin hash (ruled out, the salted SipHash stays, 4.2), the block read's zero-fill (taken, 5.2b item 6), the second walk over transactions for their size (S2 of 5.2b, nothing measurable). Pacing the prefetches is not indicated by this evidence.

### 7.10 The young table measured (the maintainer's machine, 2026-11-18)

The design is in 8.1. The maintainer's runs, `-reindex-chainstate -stopatheight=975073 -dbcache=30000 -par=16 -prevoutfetchthreads=32 -networkactive=0 -disablewallet -debug=coindb`, at tip 7abcf2575f (the series at 74e8e1db9a plus the two hint commits, which do nothing in this run, plus the two young table commits):

| `-coinsyoungtable` | wall | user | system | CPU | against 11:36.26 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1024 | 12:28.89 | 18270.93 s | 1219.59 s | 2602% | 0.930x |
| 4096 | 12:16.98 | 18353.60 s | 1211.83 s | 2654% | 0.945x |

The reference is the maintainer's earlier run of the tree of 74e8e1db9a, 11:36.26 (7.1: 17381.58 s user, 1182.99 s system); its exact options are not recorded. One run for each value. The counter lines of the two logs (ended there, lookups answered) were not read, so which share of the created coins the table kept from the main table is not known.

The reading: the table costs 6% to 7% of wall time on the full-memory run. The expectation before the run, 1.03x to 1.10x, was arithmetic on the spend ages of 7.5 and was wrong. Causes named, none measured:

- The misses it removes were hidden already. A coin spent one to six blocks after its creation had its bucket and its node touched a fraction of a second before, and the create's own probe was among the batch's overlapped prefetches.
- Every lookup pays one more probe, the lookups of old coins included, in 8 MiB (1024) or 32 MiB (4096) of slot arrays that thirty-two workers reach in no order.
- The prefetch pass of a batch reads the young slot of every item, twice, to decide what to ask for: loads that wait where before it issued prefetches only.
- A full table is moved on in one piece with the shard held, and a coin that is moved on has paid for both tables.
- A lookup counts itself in the map's header: a store on a path that only read.

### 7.11 The perf collection of 2026-11-22 (the maintainer's machine, tip cbef835ea5)

Collected with `/work/parallel-validation/perf-collect-2026-11-22.sh` during a `-reindex-chainstate` run with the usual options (9950X3D, kernel 7.0, perf 7.0.14); the text reports are in `/work/reports.tar.gz`. Collection A at 303 to 345 s of the run, collection B at 482 to 521 s. The run crossed into script checking at about 497 to 500 s: B's counters and flat profile (482 to 499 s) are still below assumevalid, B's call graphs and later steps are above it (71% of CPU in `CheckInputScripts`, 55% in `secp256k1_ecdsa_verify`). On the maintainer's word A is the reading that counts. The run's wall time is no timing. The scheduler-switch stacks and the system-call summaries were collected but not read out.

Readings from A (shares of CPU cycles unless said otherwise):

- Occupation: 28.9 of 32 hardware threads busy over ten seconds. A connect worker is on a CPU 89.5% of the time, waits for one 4.1% and sleeps 6.4% (B: 91.0, 4.6, 4.4). The connector thread (`b-initload`) is on a CPU 9% and waits for one 33% of the time. 0.62 instructions per cycle (B's counters 0.65).
- The allocator is about 25%: `_int_free_chunk` 11.8, `__libc_malloc2` 8.9, `_int_malloc` 1.7, the rest 2.3 (B's flat profile, later blocks: 14.5, 10.7, 2.9). The call sites are identified for the first time, and nearly all of it is the parsed block:
  - freeing a block (`BlockBins::Bin::Empty()`, on the thread that built it) 13.5% inclusive: `~vector<CTxIn>` 8.6 (the witness stacks), `CTransaction` 3.8;
  - allocating while parsing (`ReadBlock()`): malloc self time 7.3, of which witness items and stacks 4.7, the input vectors 1.2; witness deserialisation 6.1 inclusive.
  - So the witness stacks are about 14 to 15% of CPU here (6.1 to build, 8.6 to free). This is the subject of the separate witness pull request (not for the series).
- `ReadBlock()` 23.5% inclusive (parsing 17.9, hashing 5.2, the file read 5.6 with `_copy_to_iter` 2.9 and `kernel_init_pages` 1.3 for page cache pages); `ConnectBlock()` 25.4; `ApplyChanges()` 18.4; the continuation 15.3 (`FinishDeferredTransactions()` 11.6).
- The inputs of deferred transactions are read one at a time: `CoinsStore::LookupFor()` 7.7% inclusive, and it carries most of `pthread_mutex_unlock` and `pthread_mutex_trylock` (3.4 and 2.2 in all). The batched fetch for the continuation was on the series on 14 November and was dropped for no gain on mainnet (7.3, 8.1); the share is recorded here, the ruling stands.
- The view's own table: `HaveCoinInCache()` from `ConnectBlock()` 5.0 inclusive, `FetchCoin()` 11.8 inclusive, `SaltedCoinsCacheHasher` 3.4 (11 to 12% on 18 November), `AddCoins()` 4.7.
- Sweep candidates with a share now: S2 `SerializeTransaction<SizeComputer>` 2.5 (3.7 in B's flat profile); sigops `GetSigOpCount` 1.7; `CheckBlock()` 5.8 inclusive (S4); S1 `UpdateCoins()` 2.0 inclusive plus `SettleUndo()` 0.9, an upper bound, since the spend itself is inside; S3 is inside the witness share. S5: workers sleep 6.4%, causes not read out. S6, S7: the load-latency weight sits on the two batch lambdas as on 18 November (26.0% and 14.4%, main memory), which 7.9 reads as prefetch fill latency; nothing new. S8: `_int_free_merge_chunk` is 0.7 here, the trim path does not show.
- Load latency: 78% of the weight is served from main memory; `_copy_to_iter` 23.5% of the weight.
- Read out later the same day. System calls of the connect workers over 3 s of A (taken under `perf trace`, which slows every call, so the times are inflated and only the counts are used): about 4,700 futex calls a second per worker, a third of them returning at once because the lock word had changed; 1,550 sched_yield a second (the shard lock's try-then-yield); 67 reads a second. The untraced counters give 1,548 voluntary switches a second per worker for 6.4% asleep, about 41 microseconds a sleep: short lock waits, not waits for work. The switch stacks are cut at 2% and name one cause only: `SettleUndo()` freeing the undo data (built by the first pass's worker, freed by the continuation's) and sleeping on glibc's arena lock inside `_int_free_chunk`. One sample; which locks carry the rest is not known.

- Read out in full, 22 November, from the same reports (the per-thread switch report keeps the frames above the cut, so the shares below are sums at named frames; nothing run, nothing built; the collection is from before the plain shard mutex). No switch of a worker is a wait for a task: every one is inside a block. A worker's time, untraced: A on a CPU 89.5%, runnable without a CPU 4.1%, asleep 6.4% (1,548 voluntary switches a second, 41 microseconds each); B 91.0%, 4.6%, 4.4% (2,204 a second, 20 microseconds). The connector: on a CPU 8.9% and 7.7%, runnable without one 32.6% and 37.4%. The workers' switches in A by cause, as shares of their count: the block file read in `ReadBlock()` 27% (half of it seen down to the kernel's read); the arena lock in a free 33%, of which the undo data dropped in `SettleUndo()` 24% (a -reindex-chainstate writes no undo, so this place is the reindex's alone, S1) and the previous block freed in `BlockBins::NewBlock()` 8%; the shard lock in `LookupFor()` 16%, half of it the yield of the retry lock that is gone since; malloc under 2%; the rest under the report's cut. In the three seconds of B's switch record the process switched a tenth as often, and the workers' switches were the block file read (28%) and timer preemption inside the script checks; no lock. By time the split is not measured: `perf trace` gives 144 ms of read calls per worker in 3 s of A (4.8% of its time, the copy on the CPU included) and 17 ms in B, and futex times that tracing inflates beyond use. If most of the read time is a wait, the reads are the larger part of the sleep and the locks are under 2% of a worker's time. Which thread holds the arena lock a free waits for is not in the data.
- S5 is closed, and was closed before the sweep listed it: the maintainer's run of 20 November with a wider window showed no change (5.2a, "Window width above the default"). The sweep should not have carried it.

### 7.12 The local sync to 490,000 profiled (the maintainer's machine, 2026-11-23)

Collected with `/work/parallel-validation/perf-collect-sync.sh` during the 6:29.17 run of 7.1 (client and one serving node on the 9950X3D, 62 GB of memory, v1 transport); the output (deleted on the maintainer's word once read out; timeline every 5 s, collections at about 205,000, 351,000 and 471,000). Read out from the text reports and from the small cycles recordings binned by time; the scheduler-switch recordings (4.3 GB) were not opened.

- The sync is paced by the bytes that arrive, at every height. Block data written: 354 MB/s up to 236,000, 424 MB/s to 366,000, 488 MB/s to 445,000, 393 MB/s to 487,000; single 5 s samples from 128 to 681 MB/s. After 236,000 the client's CPU follows the byte rate (correlation 0.92, 7 to 8 core-seconds per GB) at 2.8 to 3.7 cores of 32; the serving node's CPU follows it exactly (0.996) at 0.25 to 0.31 of a core.
- Nothing in the client is occupied. At 351,000 each connect worker is on a CPU 10% of the time, at 471,000 4 to 5%; the effects thread 36% and 17%, the network thread 24% and 15%, each block I/O thread 7% and 4%, the message handler 3% and 1%, the connector 3% and 1%; no thread waits for a CPU. File work is short: writes 7 to 8 microseconds, at 471,000 four `fdatasync` a second of 28 ms and four `fsync` of 10 ms on the effects thread, `fallocate` 0.25 ms.
- The arrival is uneven below the 5 s samples. In 5 s at 364,000 to 368,000 the network thread received nothing for 2.3 s on end and the whole client stood at 0.2 cores; in 5 s at 474,000 to 477,000 arrival dropped to a trickle for about 200 ms in every 400 ms over the second half of the window. While data arrives the client uses 3 to 4 cores.
- The serving node is one busy thread, its message handler, on a CPU 20%, 33% and 19% of the time at the three heights, switching 622, 4,740 and 2,345 times a second (about 560, 125 and 180 KB of block data per switch). The client keeps at most sixteen blocks on the wire with its one peer (`BlocksToAskFor()`), and the serving node sends them one after another from that thread. What the thread waits for in the other 70 to 80% is not in the collection, which has no stacks, system calls or I/O counters of the serving node. Candidates, neither tested: reads of block files that are not in the page cache (about 145 GB of block files against 62 GB of memory, 33 GB cached at the start, with the client writing the same amount); or waits for the next request. At 205,000 it sleeps far less often than it sends blocks, which a wait for requests would not give; that favours the reads, and is no proof.
- Up to 236,000 the client burns CPU without being the bound: 436 of the run's 1,578 core-seconds in the first 32 s, 46 core-seconds per GB against 7 to 8 later, 0.39 instructions per cycle, 360,000 context switches a second; each connect worker on a CPU 51% of the time with 11,000 switches a second. `pthread_mutex_unlock` 6.3% and the kernel's spin lock slow path 3.7% of cycles at 227,000 to 243,000.
  The maintainer closed this: the phase ends at the BIP34 height (227,931), where the BIP30 checks and the spent markers of 4.6 end, and the blocks below it are tiny; the split between the two was not measured (a reindex to 236,000 with and without the BIP30 probe would give it), and it is no wall-time limit.
- The end: about 20 s from the stop height to the exit (the final write-out, 7.5 GB resident), 5% of the wall time.
- Flat profile at 471,000 (of 1.8 cores): SHA-256 12.7%, malloc and free 14.4%, `CoinsStore::CreateLocked()` 5.2%, `CompressScript()` 5.1%, the outpoint hasher 4.7%.

- The send pause is ruled out (the 6:43.88 run of 7.1): master stops serving a peer while more than `-maxsendbuffer` (1 MB) is queued for it and is not woken when the queue drains, only by a received message or after 100 ms (`net.cpp`); with the limit at 100 MB the sync took the same time and the same CPU.

- Decided the same day by the maintainer's `perf trace -s` on the serving node and `iostat -x 1` during a further sync (same options, serving node at `-maxsendbuffer=10000`, client and serving node on the one NVMe device): the serving node's message handler is inside `read` 73% of a 6.1 s trace early in the sync (52,203 calls, 4.46 s; 13,387 files opened, so 2,190 blocks a second) and 77% of a 3.6 s trace about a minute in (23,251 calls, 2.79 s, longest 13 ms; 1,627 blocks a second). Its waits for work are 2% and 0.03% (futex 136 ms and 0.9 ms), its sends 4%. It never waits for a request: the client's asking is ruled out, the block file reads are the limit. The device: one second with 427 MB/s read at 0.16 ms a request and 218 MB/s written; then, with the client's writeback at 615 to 712 MB/s (queue 100 to 113, 93 to 94% busy, 18 to 23 ms a write), reads of 67 to 92 MB/s at 1.0 to 1.3 ms a request, 54 to 60 KB each, 1,240 to 1,550 a second, so hardly more than one read in flight. The serving node opens the block file anew for every block (one `openat` per block) and reads it on one thread; the client's writes to the same device raise each read's latency six to eight times, and the read rate falls with it. The local sync on one device measures that device shared between a serial reader and the writer, not the series. Not tested: a larger `read_ahead_kb` on the device; the client's block directory or the serving node's on a second device.
- The maintainer's ruling (23 November): the serving side is the bound and is left for later; nothing on the client follows from these runs.


### 7.13 The read-only window while it was built (23 and 24 November 2026)

Dated entries in the order they were written, each of the tree it names. "The trial" is the branch the design was built on (`utxo-partial-order-overlay-trial`), "the series" in these entries is the tree of cecbce8958 or the tip named, whose window applied a block's changes into the store while the block was validated. An entry describes its own tree: several mechanisms named here were built, measured and taken off again, and the design as it stands is in 4.2, 4.5 and 4.6.

- The redesign built as a trial (23 November, on the maintainer's go, plan `/work/parallel-validation/overlay-trial-plan.md`): branch `utxo-partial-order-overlay-trial`, one commit baf938e246 on 31a9b7b0d9, not part of the series. A window block's worker parses the block and enters its transaction ids in an overlay (a table per height in a ring of 128, kept until the block a window above is published); the block is validated once every lower block in flight is indexed, with nothing deferred; a read the view does not hold looks in the overlay for the uncommitted blocks below, then in the store. The connector commits the complete prefix of the window as one batch before publishing its lowest block, in 64 shard groups claimed by itself and helpers at the front of the connect pool's queue; a coin created and spent inside a batch is dropped from both blocks (not where BIP30 is checked or fresh spends are marked). Steps 1 to 3 of the plan were built as one. Correct where checked: `feature_connect_window.py` in both forms (equal chain and UTXO hash after sync, `-reindex-chainstate` and `-reindex`; the missing input and the double spend across two window blocks rejected with master's reason, the second through a conflict and a retry alone); chain DX reindexed to the series' UTXO hash (d4ba681a..., 246,143 outputs) and passing `verifychain` level 4 over all 6,383 blocks; 0 deferred, 0 conflicts, 0 retries, 1.6 to 1.7 million inputs found in the overlay, 550,000 to 640,000 coins netted, 8.4 to 9.4 blocks a batch (12.9 at a window of 64). Harness (chain DX `-reindex-chainstate`, `-dbcache=1024`, 16 workers on 16 cores, `build-bench`, four alternating rounds, foreign load at most 1.3 cores): 509.5 against 560.6 blocks/s for the series (0.91x, ranges 495.1 to 514.4 and 550.9 to 587.4 apart), CPU 48.4 to 49.8 against 46.5 to 48.3 s, peak RSS 525 to 539 against 384 to 388 MiB. Where the time goes: the connector spends 2.1 to 2.3 s of a 4.3 s run in commits, under `cs_main`, and applies 62% of the groups itself (helpers queued at the front start only when a worker finishes its block, about 31 ms here); waiting for helpers is 0.12 s. Two probes, neither kept: the commit's own threads (seven, idle between batches) 457.1 blocks/s, the connector still applied 65% of the groups; `-connectwindow=64` 497.9 and 518.6 against 573.9 and 575.2 for the series. The netting does not pay for the second visit of each changed coin, the overlay lookups and the regrouping: more CPU in every pairing. Step 4 (commit by cell number) was not built: what it removes is mostly the commit's hash probe, and the second find per spend is already read as not paying (5.2b). The maintainer's mainnet run decides (step 5); the harness has read real gains at a third of their size before and missed a real 5% once.
- The trial taken further (23 November, after the maintainer's run of 11:47.82 in 7.1, whose user CPU was 1.009x the series and whose loss was CPU share and system time; on "Surely you can find a way to bridge that gap, no?"), a second commit c55b0c78d6 on the trial branch: the commit made by the workers, off `cs_main` (a worker that finishes a validation starts the commit of the complete blocks at the front of the window or joins it; the thread that completes a commit starts the next), the connector waiting for its front block's commit with no lock held, no spin anywhere. Alone it was slower, 440.9 blocks/s (the ordered commit is a serial chain, 4.2 s of a 5.2 s run, 83% of it the group phase, mostly on one thread). With help points in the validation (every 64 transactions a validating worker takes groups of a commit that has some left): 538.2 against 578.6 blocks/s for the series, four alternating rounds, 0.93x, CPU about 4% more, peak RSS about 190 MiB more (the overlay keeps a block a window long). A last change (help points for the views as well) was built and not measured. Stopped there on the maintainer's word ("stop the trial for now"); the branch keeps both commits. Not run on the maintainer's machine.
- The trial continued (24 November, on the maintainer's go to "steps 1 and 2" of the continuation: measure the help points for the views, then commit confirmed changes). Step 1: the help points for the views as well read 547.6 against 573.8 blocks/s for the series (0.954x; the groups alone 526.5 in the same set). Step 2, a third commit 068b16a05c on the trial branch: where nothing is netted a window block opens no slot in the store, its batch commit applies confirmed changes at its height (`CoinsStore::ApplyConfirmed()`, no record, no `ConfirmBlock()`), and its publication only moves the store's best block (`BlockApplyView::ConfirmCommitted()`); nothing writes the coins while blocks committed so are unpublished or a commit runs, a write that is due holds new commits back until they are published, and a run that started first keeps commits off until it lands (`TrialCommitGate`); a view without a slot counts as in use and is given back on every way out; under a stop a front block without a slot is committed before it is published; a commit starts only at the height above the last one committed (a race let a worker commit past a block that failed, which the window test found as a drain with a committed block in flight). Correct where checked: chain DX reindexed to the series' UTXO hash; a reindex stopped by `-stopatheight=3500` in the heavy section shuts down cleanly and a restart reaches the same hash at 6,383; the window test passes with BIP30 checked (the block-identity path) and, with BIP30 off, stops at its crafted double spend whenever both blocks are committed in one batch (four runs of five): the trial has no rollback, by its own rule, though the continuation plan had named one. Harness: 588.5 against 561.4 blocks/s (1.048x, ranges apart), and with the two fixes 609.4 against 569.7 (1.07x, ranges apart, three rounds); CPU lower than the series in every pairing (45.0 to 48.2 s against 46.3 to 49.0); peak RSS still about 180 MiB more (the overlay keeps a block a window long). The form with records read 529.0 against 542.8 in the set that measured step 2 first. The first time the trial is ahead. Not yet timed on the maintainer's machine: that run decides (the 37-patch mbox, the series at 31a9b7b0d9 and the three trial commits).
- The trial's scaling and its low end (24 November, asked whether it scales differently and has a new bottleneck). Cores, chain DX `-reindex-chainstate` at `-dbcache=1024`, workers equal to cores, two alternating rounds: 8 cores 348.3 / 349.9 against 319.6 / 332.4 blocks/s (+7%), 12 cores 477.1 / 493.4 against 458.9 / 459.1 (+5%), 16 cores 596.8 / 591.0 against 569.1 / 556.4 (+5%). The commit gets cheaper as the rate rises: 2.6 / 3.5 / 4.6 blocks a batch, 115k to 121k / 219k to 247k / 317k to 346k coins netted, 2.66 to 2.73 / 2.16 to 2.25 / 1.97 to 2.10 s in commits, about 42 / 45 / 48% of the run's wall: the ordered commit chain is the trial's first serial stage, not binding at 16 cores. The memory floor (`-dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5`) first stopped the trial after two or three runs: a spurious conflict, a spend whose coin a run's completion evicted again between its lookup and its spend (fixed in a fourth commit, 351dbb4d3c, the coin put back under the shard's lock). Then 51.9 / 51.0 against 48.0 / 47.6 blocks/s (+7%), CPU 116.8 / 120.0 against 143.4 / 145.0 s, 65 runs against 123, the series' UTXO hash, and peak RSS 647 to 680 against 451 to 476 MiB: about 200 MiB more at the floor, where the yardstick of 2026-11-17 is +78 MiB over master for the whole series (the overlay keeps a block a window long, and writes wait while the store is ahead of the tip). The mbox for the maintainer's run now holds 38 patches (the series at 31a9b7b0d9 and four trial commits).
- The trial's low-end memory (24 November, on "Do you see a fix for that?"). A probe that let each block go at its publication (unsafe, measurement only) took the peak at the floor from about 660 to 534 MiB: the overlay's retained blocks were about 130 MiB of the 190 over the series. A compact copy of the outputs in place of the block saved about 65 MiB and cost about 3% of the rate (562.6 against 581.3 blocks/s, three rounds), dropped. Kept, a fifth trial commit d2cf1effb0: the block is let go at its publication, a lookup that matched a tag counts itself in the entry while it reads the block, the release waits for the count, and a lookup that finds the entry dead reads the store. At `-dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5` 517 to 540 MiB against 648 to 672 before and 460 to 480 for the series, 51.1 / 53.2 against 47.7 / 47.3 blocks/s, the series' UTXO hash; at `-dbcache=1024` 464 to 469 against 555 to 573 MiB and 379 to 387 for the series, 586.5 / 595.3 against 562.0 / 559.5 blocks/s. What is left, about 60 MiB at the floor and 80 at 1024: the coins cache peaks at 35.8 against 24.6 MiB for the series (writes wait while the store is ahead of the tip, about 11 MiB), the rest not measured part by part (views holding changes until their commit, the commit's change lists, the overlay's 128 index tables, perhaps allocator retention from views emptied on another thread). The mbox now holds 39 patches.
- The trial's memory hunted down (24 November). Live heap equal to the series within 6 MiB at the peak; the difference was freed memory glibc kept, and several measured probes (reused buffers, views emptied by their own worker, half the window, an earlier write) did not move it. Reading the code found the cause: the window's byte account counted a block by its worker's estimate once applied, and the trial marks a block applied when indexed in the overlay, where the estimate has no inputs (about a third of a heavy block's), so the window held about three times the blocks the series holds at its byte limit. Fixed in a sixth trial commit (a block counted by its own estimate once complete). Three rounds: `-dbcache=30` peak RSS 484 to 509 against 448 to 470 MiB for the series, 43.8 to 45.1 against 46.7 to 47.7 blocks/s; `-dbcache=1024` 414 to 424 against 381 to 388 MiB, 555.4 to 590.1 against 545.1 to 579.5 blocks/s; the series' UTXO hash. The earlier leads (1.05 to 1.07x, +7% at the floor) came mostly from that wider effective window: counted alike, the trial is at parity at 1024, about 5% slower at the floor, and about 30 to 35 MiB above the series in memory. The maintainer's timing mbox before this commit carries the undercount.
- The trial's double work removed (24 November, on "Do you not see an opportunity for further optimisation and memory reduction?"), a seventh trial commit 2c2c894e35. Reading found two things the trial did twice. A window block's new coins sat in its view beside the parsed block until the batch commit (the series moves them into the store at once, where `-dbcache` counts them); and every output was added to the view during validation, then made again from the block at the commit. Now the window counts a validated block by what it holds (parsed block, undo data, commit list); a block committed as confirmed changes keeps a commit list of 16 bytes an input or output pointing into the block, made from the block and its undo data, and its view is emptied at once; and its outputs never enter the view: a later transaction of the block reads them in the window's index of the block, limited to the transactions before it, and a coin created and spent in the block shows in the undo data with the block's own height and is dropped. Blocks checked for BIP30 keep the earlier path. Chain DX `-reindex-chainstate`, 16 workers, three alternating rounds, the series' UTXO hash: at `-dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5` 60.7 / 53.1 / 60.8 against 48.9 / 48.5 / 47.6 blocks/s for the series (1.20x), CPU 109 to 125 against 141 to 146 s, peak RSS 482 to 499 against 444 to 472 MiB; at `-dbcache=1024` 570.5 / 559.7 / 570.4 against 575.1 / 563.2 / 534.0 (level), CPU 48.6 to 50.0 against 46.6 to 49.4 s, peak RSS 465 to 473 against 384 to 394 MiB. The steps between: the measured count alone 47.2 at the floor and level at 1024 with about 60 MiB more; the commit list made by view lookups 54.7 at the floor and 506 at 1024 (CPU 54.5 s); made from block and undo data 52.6 and 548; without the outputs in the view the figures above. At equal memory (`-connectwindow`, two rounds): at 1024 a window of 20 holds 387 to 390 MiB and runs 546.7 / 546.9 against 582.0 / 560.7 for the series at 380 to 386 MiB (0.96x); at the floor a window of 7 holds 449 to 454 MiB and runs 47.8 / 46.7 against 47.1 / 48.6 at 426 to 453 MiB (level), with 117 to 119 s of CPU against 141 to 145. So the trial turns memory into speed more steeply than the series (about 30 MiB for 20% at the floor) and does the floor's work with a sixth less CPU, but at the series' memory it is level at the floor and about 4% behind at 1024: a validated block waits for the ordered commit before it can be published, so the same rate needs more blocks in flight. Not built: publication ahead of the commit, which is what would take that wait away. Unit and functional tests not run on this commit; the mbox holds 41 patches.
- Where the trial's time goes besides the commit (24 November, one run each, `-debug=bench` totals, chain DX). At `-dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5` the trial uses 117.5 s of CPU in 33.4 s of wall (3.5 of 16 cores; the series 141.1 in 38.3, 3.7): the machine is mostly idle in both. The trial's commits are 8.3 s of the wall, and its connector spends 19.7 s of the 33.4 in `Writing chainstate` (the series 0.8 s: its write-outs run beside the connect, 34 s of CPU on `b-effects`; the trial's run with commits held off by `TrialCommitGate`, 11 s on `b-effects` and 10 s on the main thread). At `-dbcache=1024` a validated block waits 7.8 ms for its ordered settle in the trial against 3.7 ms in the series (the bench line `Queue undo data`, validation's end to the settle), with equal CPU in that pair (48.6 against 48.1 s). So: at the floor the write-out shutting out commits and publication is the first limit, not the commit; at 1024 it is the wait for the ordered commit, which is memory rather than rate.

The 32-bit links are in the store and inherited. Trialled: (1) and (2), ninth trial commit 0281133b5b (a flat tag table kept by the thread, a match checked against the outpoint). No holder of tens of MiB was found; the unattributed rest is taken to be what the allocator keeps of these large short-lived buffers on sixteen threads, which is an inference, not a finding.

A correction of scale first: the heavy section of chain DX has about 3000 transactions a block, not 300 (the log's transaction count rises by 1,800,600 over heights 2999 to 3599), a block is 1.6 MB as stored and by the sizes of its parts about 3.7 MiB parsed, and the coin set stays at 26 MiB (246,000 coins) for the whole run. Not allocated on the trial's path: store records and plain creates (`ReserveRecord()` is reached only for a block with a slot), `StagedConnect::changes` and its flags, the announce table. Candidates of the size of the gap, neither confirmed: (1) a published block is freed only when the thread that parsed it parses again (`BlockBins`); in the series every task begins with a parse, in the trial a worker runs validations handed to the front of the queue and commit groups between parses, so freed blocks wait longer, 3.7 MiB each; (2) parse and validation are separate steps, so a worker whose block waits for a lower one to be indexed parses the next, and more blocks are parsed and not yet validated than the series' one per worker. From the reviewer's retrospective, not built: the overlay without coin copies (the trial copies each window-made input into the view, 3.1 million in the run; the view is not larger than the series' for it, which holds the outputs too, so it is a gain below the series rather than the gap); commits of 16 to 32 blocks by the connector (the trial's are 2.81 blocks, and net 304,000 coins of 3.1 million window-made inputs; a speed lever, and it holds validated blocks longer); database reads not left in the store as clean entries (the trial keeps them; no effect at a cache the set fits in). From the findings report: the double find in `ApplyBatch()` is in `ApplyConfirmed()` too (speed only); the allocator items concern address space and reclamation, the same in both builds. Redesign 2 stays excluded by the ruling on bytes per coin.

With that the trial's case rests on the simpler design alone, which it has not realised (nothing deleted, no invalid-block path).
